##### S U M M A R Y.

1–4. The use of external, visible, and emblematic things to rise toward invisible and interior things is of divine institution <note>established by God</note>; it is easy, pleasant, and suited to the capacity of everyone. Several examples of its utility. 5–7. Emblems intended to act upon the heart are preferable to those which only awaken the mind; the path of the heart being much better than that of speculation <term>speculation: abstract intellectual study or theorizing</term>, according to the word of God himself. 8. 9. Some specific details regarding the Emblems of Father Hugo <note>Herman Hugo (1588–1629), a Jesuit priest famous for his devotional book "Pia Desideria"</note> & Vænius <note>Otto van Veen (c. 1556–1629), a Flemish painter and teacher of Rubens known for his book of "Emblems of Divine Love"</note>, their subject, and various of their previous editions. 10–12. Concerning this new edition, the new verses inserted within it, and their

Although God is pure spirit, and the principal part of man is also spirit, and the essential of divine worship—the adoration that God demands of us—must be done in the spirit and in the interior, as <note>original: "Jesus-Christ même"</note> Jesus Christ himself assures us (a); nevertheless, since men after the sin <note>Referring to the Fall of Man in Genesis</note> have become entirely focused on the exterior, and having fallen into the sensible and the visible they have forgotten the invisible and the spiritual; it has pleased God, in order to raise them from this fall, to condescend to their coarse disposition even to the point of using these same visible and sensible things as means to bring them back to the divine and interior things for which they were created. Everything that our eyes discover in the works of Creation can be employed for this salutary use according to the intention of God himself and this assertion of St. Paul (b), that the invisible things of God, his

*power and his divine goodness, are seen as if painted before our eyes when one considers his works; and that if we do not draw from them a reason to praise and glorify him, it is to make ourselves guilty of a criminal and inexcusable negligence. Most of what is prescribed by the Law of Moses <note>The Torah or Pentateuch, the first five books of the Bible.</note> regarding Jewish worship is properly nothing but a use of various external and visible things established by God to signify the invisible and the interior. How many times did Jesus Christ and his Holy Apostles make use of EMBLEMS <term>Emblems: symbolic images accompanied by text used to convey a moral or religious truth.</term> and parables drawn from natural things, from artificial things, even from civil matters and from what is practiced in matters of government, war, peace, contracts, friendship, conjugal love, etc., in order to raise our spirits and our hearts from there to the consideration and the love of the things of the spirit, of heaven, and of eternity? Examples of this present themselves in crowds within the Holy Scripture.*

*2. This method—of bringing back to spiritual things our minds which have fallen upon the sensible and the material—comes to us therefore from the goodness of God, and from the condescension of his Wisdom toward our weakness; there is no doubt that it should be as commendable to us for its salutary utility, as it is eas—*

# P R E F A C E

pleasant, and proportioned to the capacity of all sorts of people.

3. And indeed, there are even children into whom one can instill—with fruit, with pleasure, and even by way of entertainment—pious thoughts concerning God and their duty toward him. This is done by placing before their eyes certain figures or representations of several common things toward which their hearts and minds have a natural inclination; from there, it is easy to impress upon them how they must turn this same inclination toward God, the Creator of all things, and in particular their Creator and also their Redeemer.

As for adults, how many have there been for whom the sight of something visible served as the occasion for their conversion? God, having used those means, made such vivid and powerful impressions on their hearts that they found themselves suddenly changed. Even for the rest of their lives, every time the simple idea returned to them, they felt themselves entirely moved within and newly revived. We are told of a simple soldier, who afterwards became one of the holiest souls, and whose Life and several letters have recently been published: <margin>(a) See *The Manners of Brother Lawrence*, in the small treatise *The Theology of the Presence of God*, page 57. <note>Brother Lawrence (c. 1614–1691) was a lay brother in a Carmelite monastery in Paris, famous for his simple approach to spirituality through "the practice of the presence of God."</note></margin> *That a tree*

4. As for more advanced souls, and even the most perfect ones, who already find and see God everywhere and in all things, one need only read the Psalms of David <note>The Book of Psalms in the Old Testament, traditionally attributed to King David.</note> to notice how much this Holy Prophet felt instructed, touched, revived, and ravished with admiration and ineffable joy when he contemplated visible things. He took these as occasions to raise himself to God, viewing them as paintings that represented to him His supreme grandeur, His wisdom, His goodness, and things divine and spiritual. The wisest of men, his son Solomon, did no less when he employed the consideration of human and conjugal love to depict for us, under this Emblem, the greatest and most interior mysteries of the spiritual union of perfected souls and the sanctified Church with the heavenly Spouse; as appears by his divine Song of Songs <note>Also known as the Canticle of Canticles, a book of the Bible often interpreted as an allegory of the soul's relationship with God.</note>.

5. It is to be believed that it is through similar considerations, and with the intent of providing some salutary utility to all sorts of persons, that there have appeared from time to time BOOKS OF SPIRITUAL EMBLEMS <note>original: "LIVRES D'EMBLEMES SPIRITUELS"</note>

<summary>The author discusses how spiritual figures like David and Solomon used the physical world and human love as emblems to contemplate divine truths, justifying the use of emblem books for spiritual growth.</summary>
<keywords>Psalms of David, Solomon, Song of Songs, spiritual emblems, divine contemplation, allegory, soul's union with God</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the preface's discussion on spiritual methodology, contrasting intellectual study with the devotion of the heart. Catchword from previous page: "the a-" (referring to "actual" or "active" states).</meta>

->on this Edition.<-

ACTUAL <note>In this context, "actual" refers to active spiritual exercises or present-moment states of grace.</note>, which under the veil of various figures piously attempt to turn our souls toward God; some by imprinting upon our minds certain ideas or considerations that lead us to think of Him, others by awakening in our HEART affective movements <note>"Affective" refers to the realm of feelings, desires, and the will, rather than pure logic.</note> which carry us toward LOVING HIM and toward holily seeking union with Him and His perfect and eternal possession. This method is incomparably preferable to that of simple speculation, despite the opinion of most scholars who, despising the way of the heart, persuade themselves—quite vainly—that by the way of a dry spirit, and by employing and exhausting all their activity and all the forces of their reason in ideas and reasonings on divine things, they might better find God than by the way of exercising our heart in His divine Love.

6. Without appealing to the experience of all times, which shows us how little fruit the spirit of man has produced by the way of his cold speculations, the sole testimony of God must suffice for us to decide this question. It is incontestably certain that God has promised His divine and salutary knowledge and His beatific <note>original: "béatifique"; referring to the supreme happiness of heaven and the direct vision of God.</note> union to those who seek Him by the way of the heart and of

<summary>The author argues that seeking God through the "way of the heart" and divine love is far superior to the "dry" intellectual speculation favored by scholars. He asserts that God's promises of union are intended for those who approach Him through affection rather than mere reason.</summary>
<keywords>spirituality, way of the heart, divine love, speculation vs. affection, beatific union, religious methodology</keywords>

# XIII
-> on this Edition. <-

Authors of the following Emblems. Not everyone is capable of proceeding by way of the head and intellectual speculations; but everyone has a heart, a penchant for loving, inclinations, movements, and lively affections, which one cannot prevent from acting and exercising themselves upon objects—whether good or bad, temporal or eternal—that are proposed to us. It is up to us to choose between these two sides, each of which solicits our love to join its ranks: Satan and the world toward the side of evil by a thousand kinds of attractions, even by an infinity of vain, impious, and impure books, and by profane, shameful, and diabolical images and paintings; God, on the contrary, draws us toward the good by His <term>good movements<note>Refers to "internal graces" or divine impulses felt within the soul to perform good acts.</note></term> and by other sacred and salutary means. Happy is he who makes the right choice, and who allows himself to be led, as if by the hand, to the source of true happiness by the means that God presents to him! One can surely regard the two works in this book as being among the number of those good means.

8. The first rank has been given to that of Father *Herman Hugo* <note>Herman Hugo (1588–1629) was a Jesuit priest and author of the "Pia Desideria," one of the most popular spiritual emblem books of the 17th century.</note>, although it is the more recent, because it is the most methodical, and because its first emblems particularly concern <term>beginning souls<note>A term from mystical theology referring to those in the "purgative way," the first stage of spiritual life focused on turning away from sin.</note></term>. There has long—

# PREFACE

Since this work is so well known, it is almost superfluous to mention that it has been reprinted several times and in various places, with explanations of its <term>Emblems<note>In this context, an "emblem" is a symbolic illustration accompanied by a moral or spiritual poem and a motto.</note></term> in all sorts of languages. It is divided into three parts: the first, intended for beginners, contains *the groans of the penitent soul*; the second, which is for the use of advanced souls, represents *the desires of a soul being sanctified*; and the third, proportioned to those who have made the most progress, has as its title and subject, *the sighs of the loving soul*. Each of these three parts contains fifteen Emblems; each emblem, in the Latin original, has its own particular illustration; then a passage from Holy Scripture, marking in a few words what this Emblem represents; which, thirdly, is followed by a fairly large number of Latin verses on the same subject; and finally, several passages from the <term>Holy Fathers<note>The "Holy Fathers" (SS. Péres) refers to the influential early Christian theologians and writers of the Church.</note></term> and the Doctors of the Church, applicable to the matter at hand. Those who have had the work reprinted in various common languages have not, however, felt obliged to stick to all of that, but only to what is essential and principal: and for this reason, they have retained the Emblems with their illustrations, which they have had imitated or copied in various—

<summary>This preface page describes the structure of a popular devotional emblem book, divided into three spiritual stages: repentance, sanctification, and divine love. It explains that while the original Latin version included extensive verses and patristic citations, vernacular translations often focus on the essential illustrations and core messages.</summary>
<keywords>Emblem books, spiritual progress, penitence, sanctification, divine love, translation history, Holy Fathers, devotional literature</keywords>

# On this Edition.

<margin>catchword: on</margin>

ment, some well, some poorly. They have retained, in the second place, and translated each into their own language all the passages of Holy Scripture: But no one, as far as I know, has yet found it appropriate to apply themselves to the translation of the Latin verses that were annexed to them: everyone has preferred to try their hand at being a poet here, and compose from their own head some verses (some more and others less) on the subject of each emblem. All (as many as I have seen) have omitted the passages from the Holy Fathers <note>The "Holy Fathers" refers to the Patristic authors, the influential early theologians of the Christian Church.</note>, whether they regarded them as a pure accessory to the work, as they are in effect; or whether they intended by this means to make the book more convenient and more portable. This latter consideration has not, nonetheless, prevented us from joining to the Emblems of Father Hugo <note>Herman Hugo (1588–1629), a Jesuit priest and author of the immensely popular "Pia Desideria."</note> those of *Otto Vaenius* <note>original: "Othon Venius"; the Latinized name of Otto van Veen, a Flemish painter and teacher of Peter Paul Rubens.</note>; since without making the volume too large, they visibly belong to this same subject, of which they more amply display the noblest part, which is that of divine LOVE.

9. It is known that this Flemish Author, a celebrated painter, and one who was well-studied, had published in his youth moral Emblems on natural love. Some years later, the Princess Infanta Isabella, Duchess of Brabant, who had seen them, expressed a wish that he had worked in the same manner

# PREFACE

on **DIVINE LOVE**; since it was easy to discover and to demonstrate in the one, as in the other, similar qualities and effects; this led him to undertake these **Emblems**, which he dedicated to the same Princess. Opposite each figure, he placed a few words as an inscription, along with several maxims from either the **Holy Scriptures** or the **Church Fathers** <note>original: "des Péres"</note> which relate to them; to which his friends added verses—though very few—some in Spanish and others in French and Flemish. This is how they first appeared, *(a)* <margin>The year 1615.</margin> a few years before the Emblems *(b)* <margin>Which appeared in the year 1624.</margin> of **Father Hugo** <note>Herman Hugo (1588–1629), a Jesuit priest famous for his "Pia Desideria," a highly influential book of spiritual emblems.</note>. Those who subsequently had them published in various other places retained the design of the figures, which they had imitated—some quite well, as in the Paris edition by **Landry** <note>Pierre Landry (1630–1701), a prominent Parisian engraver and publisher.</note>: they also retained the sayings or inscriptions, but without the passages from the Scriptures or the Holy Fathers. As for the verses, everyone included some of their own fashioning—just as was done with Father Hugo—and still very few: the Paris Edition has only four small lines for each Emblem. This was a matter of choice; therefore, for the same reason, we have acted by our own choice in this present Edition, regarding which it is time to offer a word of advice.

# XVII
->on this Edition.<-
**

10. One sees here, first of all, all the emblematic figures of Father *Hugo* <note>Herman Hugo (1588–1629), a Jesuit priest famous for his "Pia Desideria," a book of spiritual emblems.</note> & of *Vænius* <note>Otto van Veen (c. 1556–1629), also known as Vaenius, a painter and teacher of Rubens known for his emblem books.</note>, which have been imitated from the most excellent originals of the best editions of these two authors. Their beauty and the softness of their engraving create a sufficiently fine effect to be, if not preferred, at least equaled to the best of those which have appeared until now in any edition whatsoever. We have also retained the passages of Holy Scripture <note>original: "l’Ecriture sainte"</note> which were upon the emblems of Father *Hugo*, and the Latin sayings or mottos <note>original: "Dictons ou mots latins"</note> from those of *Vænius*, which have been translated into English <note>original: "mis en françois"</note> on the pages facing the figures, and immediately before the new verses which express their meaning.

11. It is properly to these divine and admirable verses, as much on the emblems of *Vænius* as on those of Father *Hugo*, that we are indebted for the present edition; and assuredly it is they which most deserve that the reader apply his heart to them very seriously. I describe them as I have just done, not so much in relation to the simple poetry—which nevertheless has its charms and a very vivid and touching beauty—as in relation to their entirely holy subject matter, and to their spirit, which truly is divine and from heaven. It is here that it appears to us that the poet has very often surpassed the design and the thoughts of our

fall by that same Love, and by the truth of those words of love: (a) *Lord, you who know all things, you know that I love you:* <note>original: "Seigneur, qui savez toutes choses, vous savez que je vous aime" (John 21:17). # P R E F A C E

...still the path of the holy Religious of Brittany, **JOHN OF ST. SAMSON** <note>Jean de Saint-Samson (1571–1636) was a blind Carmelite friar and mystic known as the "French John of the Cross."</note>, who, despite being entirely blind from childhood, nevertheless walked this noble career without stumbling, and left behind a great number of Treatises full of ardor and divine unction, all of which he dictated by that same Love. Then there is the path of the good **BROTHER LAWRENCE OF THE RESURRECTION** <note>Nicolas Herman (c. 1614–1691), a lay brother famous for his "Practice of the Presence of God."</note>, whose conversion was mentioned shortly before; and finally that of the admirable **ARMELLE NICOLAS**, called "Good Armelle," a poor, uneducated <term>idiote</term> <note>In this 18th-century context, "idiote" refers to someone who is unlettered or of humble, simple status, rather than a modern medical definition.</note> peasant and servant girl. Her heart and spirit, her actions and speech, breathed nothing but the pure Love of God, who caused her to experience and undergo the most marvelous of His operations, and who led her to say on this subject:

> (a) *O my* **LOVE** *and my* **ALL**, *who would have ever thought to see this heart in the state it is in now? 12. It is to similar operations of **Divine Love** <term>Divine Love: often referring to 'Caritas' or 'Agape', the selfless love of God for humanity and humanity's reciprocal devotion</term>, entirely noble and generous, entirely pure and disinterested, and which regards only **God Alone**, its true and its unique object, its motive, its end, and its **All**, that the sublime explanations return—those which have been given to the following Emblems <note>Emblems: symbolic illustrations accompanied by moral or spiritual poems, popular in the 17th century</note> in the verses which are annexed to them, and which seem to be nothing but ardent effusions of a heart entirely animated and moved by the purest Love of God, and almost continual elevations of this same heart to God. If I dared to venture my thoughts or my conjectures regarding their Author, I would say—

# PREFACE

kings, that if it is not the same person who recently published several (a) Volumes of *Explanations and Reflections on the Old and New Testament regarding the Interior Life* <note>The "Interior Life" refers to the mystical practice of quietism and inner prayer</note>, it must at least be someone endowed with the same spirit and the same dispositions of heart; since nothing is easier than to notice here the same principles and the same element of pure Love <term>Pure Love: the disinterested love of God for His own sake, without regard for reward or punishment</term> as in those excellent *Explanations and Reflections on the Holy Scripture*. We nevertheless leave to those who have read, or who wish to read and compare these different works together, the freedom to judge as they see fit. We only advise them that the Author of these verses (b) having composed them on two different occasions based on the Emblems of Vaenius <note>Otto van Veen, also known as Vaenius, was a Flemish painter and teacher of Rubens known for his emblem books</note>, we have placed the latter of these compositions separately toward the end of the work, without intending for it to be regarded as inferior to the first: the only difficulty found in placing both among the illustrations is the reason we had to proceed in this manner.

13. In conclusion, we wish for all those who desire to make good use of this book the disposition of soul that is necessarily required for this purpose. This disposition is clearly depicted in all the figures of these Emblems under the form of a child; which signifies that the soul wishing to enter into and persevere in communication with God and His divine Love must be endowed with the lovely and childlike qualities of innocence, simplicity, purity, self-detachment <note>original: "desapropriation" — a mystical term referring to the letting go of self-will and personal ownership of one's actions</note>, candor, kindness, docility, and a flexibility to allow oneself to be led and governed by God like a little child, without reluctance, without presumption, without pride, without malice, without fraud, and without duplicity of heart. This is what the word of God itself requires of us more than once through the mouths of *David*, *Solomon*, *Isaiah*, and the Prophets; the Apostles *St. Peter*, *St. Paul*, *St. John*; and finally *Jesus Christ*, who assures us that (*a*) *the Kingdom of God is for those who are like children*: that *if one does not wish to receive it in the disposition of a child, one shall not enter it*, and that one will not even have true knowledge of it, since (*b*) *the Father only makes known His Son and the mysteries of His Kingdom to the simple and the little ones*, according to the assertion

---

(*a*) Mark 10:14, 15. (*b*) Matthew 11:25.

# PROLOGUE.

There are here three kinds of sighs:
The first are the effect of a profound sorrow,
For having so greatly offended the Creator of the world:
The heart is overwhelmed by cruel displeasures;
->To satisfy Justice,<-
->One imposes a certain penance,<-
->One works to correct oneself;<-
This is the first means to make us change.
He whose goodness for us is without equal
Appears in order to console this heart,
->When, in ceasing to be a sinner,<-
->It annihilates and humbles itself:<- <note>The French "se ravale" implies a voluntary lowering of one's status or ego before the Divine.</note>
God, who delights in our humility,
->Fills the heart with charity:<- <note>In this context, "charity" refers to *caritas*, or divine love.</note>
These are other sighs, which come from a flame
Much purer, and already our soul
->Can sigh only with love.<-
These sighs go toward God, and even without detour:
For the first sighs, curved back upon ourselves,
->Seemed to look only at us:<-
One feared even the slightest blows from my God:
The pain and the sorrow which seemed extreme to us
->Envisaged only one's own interest,<-
->One feared the divine decree:<-
->The sighs of the loving soul<-
Rise straight to the Lord: Yes, I am willing to perish
->If my loss is glorious to you,<-
She says, and God, make me die soon.

<summary>This prologue introduces a spiritual hierarchy of "sighs" or prayers, moving from the sorrowful repentance of a sinner fearing punishment to the selfless, ecstatic love of a soul seeking union with God.</summary>
<keywords>repentance, divine justice, humility, charity, divine love, spiritual transformation, sighs</keywords>

# XXVIII.

### PROLOGUE.

This love, however, is mingled with sorrow;
   One is pained by having offended Him,
   One desires vengeance for it,
One even wishes that God would not spare the heart:
->Punish, punish, my adorable Master,<-
->This heart as ungrateful as it is treacherous.<-

Then comes a certain sigh of love:
   How delightful is this sigh!
For the soul no longer feels the sorrow that overwhelms it;
   It dwells in another abode:
One does nothing more than languish upon the earth,
   One would wish to pass into one's God:
   The activity of this beautiful fire
Is to ascend again to its sphere <note>In the cosmology of the time, fire naturally sought to return to its "sphere" above the earth; here it serves as a metaphor for the soul returning to God.</note>.

   Little by little the sighs are extinguished,
   One no longer knows how to sigh,
   One no longer knows how to desire,
It seems that these fires, once so charming, restrain themselves.

No, no, they have passed into the tranquility
Of a fire that no subject <note>In this context, "subject" refers to earthly attachments or material things that might hold the soul back.</note> retains in this world,
   They cross the earth and the wave
      To lose themselves in unity.

<keywords>mysticism, divine love, soul's journey, tranquility, spiritual fire, unity with God</keywords>

## DEDICATION
# TO JESUS
### The Desired One.

> *Lord, all my desire is laid bare before your eyes;*
> *and my groaning is not hidden from you.* <note>A translation of Psalm 38:9 (Vulgate Psalm 37:10), a common devotional opening expressing total transparency before God.</note>

I sigh toward you, O my only Good!
The sigh is the heart's faithful interpreter;
Though my tongue may be mute,
The language of the heart reaches even to you.
You, who know well the secret of my soul,
Do not reject my sighs:
As they escape, they redouble my flame,
And soften my displeasures.
Ever-watchful Eye, adorable Wisdom <note>original: "Sapience," a term for Divine Wisdom often personified in mystical literature.</note>,
Nothing can be hidden from you;
You see the evil that overwhelms me,
Even though my heart is detached from everything.
In this sacred desert <note>A metaphorical reference to the "desert" of the soul or a life of monastic seclusion.</note> I sigh without ceasing:
I recognize well, however,
That these sighs come from my weakness,
And are not befitting the most perfect Lover <note>original: "Amant," used here in the mystical sense of the soul as the lover of God.</note>.

<summary>A poetic dedication to Jesus, framing the author's spiritual journey as a "language of the heart" expressed through sighs and longing within a sacred solitude.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, Jesus, Divine Wisdom, mysticism, spiritual longing, sacred desert, soul's lover</keywords>

-># EMBLEMS<-

## BOOK ONE.

### I.

> *My soul has desired you during the night.* <note>original: "Mon ame vous a désiré pendant la nuit." A reference to Isaiah 26:9, often used in mystical theology to describe the soul's longing for God.</note>

OF two kinds of nights where one seeks the Spouse <note>The "Spouse" refers to Christ, following the tradition of the Song of Solomon where the soul is the Bride.</note>,
The first begins the journey:
By the favor of its light,
One leaves behind the sin that seemed too sweet:
The soul sees clearly then that it walks in darkness;
And this effect of a small glimmer
Makes conversions celebrated;
This weak clarity comes, nonetheless, from love.
There is another night; but a night entirely divine;
Where neither lamp nor torch appears;
It is the purest Love which illuminates itself,
And gives us a new state.
O dark faith <note>original: "ténébreuſe foi." This refers to the "Dark Night of the Soul," a concept where the soul's sensory and intellectual understandings are darkened so it can unite more deeply with God.</note>, you are preferable
To that which we call clarity:
You allow us to enjoy that immutable All
Which grants true happiness.

# BY H. HUGO.
<note>Herman Hugo (1588–1629) was a Jesuit priest and famous author of "Pia Desideria," a book of "godly desires" or emblems that became a spiritual bestseller in the 17th century.</note>

<sig>A 2</sig>

## II.

> *O God, you know my foolishness, and my sins are not hidden from you.*
<note>original: "O Dieu, vous connoissez ma folie, & mes péchés ne vous sont point cachés." This is a translation of Psalm 69:5.</note>

How unhappy I was, when far from you
I loved only vain things!
Ranking myself there among the fools,
My steps then seemed certain to me:
I went astray at every moment
Within these vain amusements,
Which I dared indeed to call wisdom:
Divine Love, you come to call me
You pull me from my weakness,
You attract my heart and deign to speak to it:
Ah, I did not listen to that charming voice
Which spoke in the depths of my soul;
To follow my unworthy choice
I dared to hide myself from your sweet flame:
I was a horror to you, and I applauded myself
In secret within my folly:
How I regret it! see my repentance:
It is you, divine Love, who will change my life,
You alone can convert me.

<summary>This page contains a poetic meditation on Psalm 69, where the speaker laments their past worldly "wisdom" as mere folly and calls upon Divine Love for conversion. It reflects the 17th-century "Emblem" tradition of personal, emotional dialogue with God.</summary>

<keywords>Herman Hugo, Divine Love, repentance, spiritual folly, Jesuit spirituality, 17th-century poetry, conversion</keywords>

# E M B L E M S

### III.

> *Have mercy on me, Lord, because I am weak:*
> *Lord, heal me, because*
> *my bones are all shaken.* <note>A translation of Psalm 6:2, a common prayer of penance and physical/spiritual distress.</note>

Have mercy on me, my adorable Master;
->My body is weak and languishing!<-
->Every moment destroys my being:<-
You alone can heal me, O my celestial Lover! <note>The term "Amant" (Lover) was frequently used in mystical literature to describe the intense, personal relationship between the soul and God.</note>

Ah, the sickness within is more unbearable to me
->Than the pains my body suffers;<-
->If I could only be pleasing to you<-
->I would laugh at outward evils.<-

Heal, change my heart; how happy I shall be
To endure a thousand different torments every day!
->If I can be your Beloved<-
->I will defy the entire universe.<-

I no longer fear boredom or pain,
->If I belong to my Love;<-
->If I could only wear his chain,<-
I would lose the light of day without regret.

# FROM H. HUGO <note>Herman Hugo (1588–1629) was a Jesuit priest and author of the famous emblem book "Pia Desideria," which used poetry and imagery to guide the soul toward God.</note>

### IV.

->*Look upon the humble and painful state in which I*<-
->*find myself; and forgive me all my sins.*<-

I acknowledge my iniquity <note>original: "iniquité" — a term for gross injustice or wickedness.</note>
And the magnitude of my offense:
Look upon my penance,
And treat me, Lord, according to your will.

I shall not complain of such painful labor,
I would wish to suffer more evils
If I could, through my labors,
Make you feel my pain.

Ah, what am I saying, Lord? Strike, double your blows;
Do not spare this rebellious heart
Since it deserves your wrath <note>original: "couroux" — an archaic spelling of "courroux," meaning intense anger or divine indignation.</note>,
Ah, strike and make it more faithful.

I detest this ungrateful heart.
I love my punishment, I find it just:
And under the labor that casts me down
I secretly bless the blows with which it overwhelms me.

Ah, redouble my evils; erase my sin,
That, dear Lover <note>In the tradition of mystical poetry, the soul often addresses God or Christ as the "Lover" or "Beloved."</note>, is all that I ask:
Never be moved by my labor;
Your wrath alone is what I fear:
If I please you, all torments
Will bring me contentment.

<sig>A 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: V. Un-</meta>

<summary>A poetic meditation on repentance where the speaker begs for divine punishment to purify their rebellious heart and find favor with God.</summary>
<keywords>Herman Hugo, penance, divine wrath, repentance, mystical poetry, Jesuit spirituality, atonement</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
### V.

->*Remember, I pray you, that you have made me*<-
->*like a work of clay; and that in a short*<-
->*time you will reduce me to dust.*<-
<note>This is a paraphrase of Job 10:9, reflecting on the fragility of human life.</note>

You have, my Lord, formed me from a little ash,
    And I am soon to return there:
Far from raising myself up, I must always descend;
To contempt, to sorrows I wish to abandon myself.
    O my only hope in my long misery,
        In forming me in your fashion
        Impress upon me this lesson,
        That I am nothing but dust!
How could I be carried away by any pride
    Knowing well my origin?
    If I immerse myself in my nothingness
I shall re-enter the divine Essence.
My simple and pure spirit emanates from my God;
    My body has come forth from the earth:
    Let each return to its place,
The body into dust, and the soul into its sphere.
O sovereign Love, transport my spirit,
    And immerse it in its principle!
Make it so that my body, being reduced to dust,
May one day participate in the happiness of the spirit!

# E M B L E M S

### V.

> *Remember, I pray you, that you have made me*
> *like a work of clay; and that in a short*
> *time you will reduce me to dust.*
<note>This is a paraphrase of Job 10:9 from the Old Testament.</note>

**Y**ou have, my Lord, formed me from a little ash,
->And I am soon to return there:<-
Far from raising myself up, I must always descend;
To contempt, to sorrows, I wish to abandon myself.
->O my only hope in my long misery,<-
->In forming me in your fashion<-
->Impress upon me this lesson,<-
->That I am nothing but dust!<-
Could I carry myself away with any pride
->Knowing well my origin?<-
->If I immerse myself in my nothingness<-
I shall re-enter the divine Essence.
My simple and pure spirit emanates from my God;
->My body has come forth from the earth:<-
->Let each return to its place,<-
The body into powder, and the soul into its sphere.
O sovereign Love, transport my spirit,
->And immerse it in its principle!<-
Make it so that my body, being reduced to dust,
May one day participate in the happiness of the spirit!

<summary>A poetic meditation on human mortality and the soul's divine origin, drawing on the biblical imagery of man being formed from clay and returning to dust. The author expresses a desire for spiritual humility and eventual reunification with the "divine Essence."</summary>
<keywords>mortality, creation, clay and dust, divine essence, humility, soul and body, sovereign love</keywords>

# FROM H. HUGO <note>Herman Hugo (1588–1629) was a Jesuit priest whose book "Pia Desideria" became the most famous devotional emblem book of the 17th century.</note>

## VI.

> *I have sinned: what shall I do to appease you, O Sav-*
> *ior of men? Why have you set me*
> *in a state contrary to you.* <note>original: "Peccavi: quid faciam tibi, o custos hominum? quare posuisti me contrarium tibi." This is a paraphrase of Job 7:20.</note>

I have resisted you, pure & divine Love,
I have resisted you; how great was my audacity!
->Ah, can I still endure the light of day?<-
No; it is only with trembling that I ask for grace.
With all my heart I submit myself to you,
->It is done, I surrender my weapons to you;<-
->Unworthy of your wrath,<-
->I hope for nothing from my tears.<-
You have disarmed me, O too charming Victor,
->I must be your captive;<-
->You have carried away my heart;<-
I no longer fear that it will ever happen to me,
->Divine Love, to fight against you.<-
To prevent this evil I give myself up without pretense:
->My soul has lost all fear,<-
->And wishes to expose itself to your blows:<-
Punish, forgive, you are the master of it.
These blows coming from you will make my heart hap-
->py:<-
->This heart would be a coward, a traitor,<-
If your punishment seemed rigorous to it.
->You are the author of its being,<-
And you have made it too full of love.

# E M B L E M S

### V I I.

->*Why do you hide your face from me, and why do you consider me your enemy?* <note>A paraphrase of Job 13:24, expressing the soul's distress at feeling abandoned by God.</note><-

->*The Soul.*<-

Ah, hide your lovely face from me no longer!
I cannot endure this cruel punishment:
It is to punish me far more
Than to deliver me to the harshest torment.
Holy and sacred Love <note>original: "Amour saint & sacré" — referring here to God as Divine Love</note>, have you no other pains?
Rather, deliver me to your fires:
Inflict upon my body the most terrible tortures <term>gênes: an archaic term for judicial torture or extreme physical distress</term>;
But do not steal your charms from my eyes.
Alas, divine Love, I am punished enough,
Let me see you for a moment;
If not, I am going to lose my life,
Take pity on me, dear Lover!

->*Our Lord.*<-

Do you not see, too indiscreet Lover,
That you cannot yet see me?
Is your heart without desire and without inclination <note>original: "pante" (pente) — meaning a leaning or tendency toward worldly things</note>?
Is it submitted to my will?
Importune me no more, and suffer my absence
To punish you for your error
And for your foolish resistance:
To see me, you must better purify your heart:
You must abandon yourself,
And let me act according to my pleasure.
If you loved me as I wish to be loved,
You would not dare to form a single desire.

# VIII.

> *Who will give water to my head, and to my eyes*
> *a fountain of tears, to weep*
> *day and night?*
<note>original: "Qui donnera de l'eau à ma tête, & à mes yeux une fontaine de larmes, pour pleurer jour & nuit?" This is a translation of Jeremiah 9:1.</note>

Just as an alembic <term>alembic: a chemical apparatus used for distilling liquids, here used as a metaphor for the heart</term> the heat of love
Dissolves the heart and distills it into tears;
If it does not melt away each day,
It is hardly smitten by His charms.

This is the first effect produced by this beautiful fire:
But a more ardent fire causes the Lover himself to pass
Into the heart of this God whom he loves;
Then there is no longer any middle ground
Between this Lover and his God.

Weep, my eyes, weep, change yourselves into a foun-
tain,
In order to make me obtain
This sovereign charity <note>In this context, "charity" refers to *Caritas*, the highest form of divine love.</note>
Which alone can unite me to my God.

# E M B L E M S

### I X.

->*I was besieged by the pains of hell, and the snares*<-
->*of death were laid before me.*<-
<note>This is a paraphrase of Psalm 18:5 (or Psalm 116:3), a common theme in 17th-century devotional literature expressing the soul's desperation.</note>

**W**retched that I am, to what state do I see myself reduced?
Death, and the hell that drags me down,
Show me my certain ruin
Without me being able to see where death leads me.
Dying, I am caught in its nets,
My soul is already a prisoner;
The hell that holds me in its snares
Does not allow me even to hope.
Great God, come to my aid;
If not, I am near to perishing.

I perceive my Savior with a helping hand
Who comes at this moment to break my bonds:
How favorable this help is to me!
Reviving my hope, He grants me a thousand blessings.
Alas, draw me out of myself,
And I shall no longer fear either hell or death:
If some day my heart loves You,
I will laugh at their efforts.

Pardon my transgression, grant that I may follow You,
O my powerful Liberator!
And if it is Your will that I live,
Let it be, then, for Your honor!

<meta>catchword: X. Not</meta>

<summary>Emblem IX depicts the soul's transition from the despair of being trapped by death and hell to the hope found in the Savior's intervention. It concludes with a prayer for liberation and a life dedicated to God's honor.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual warfare, salvation, Psalm 18, divine mercy, liberation, repentance</keywords>

# OF H. HUGO.

### X.

> Do not enter into judgment with your servant. <note>original: "N'entrez point en jugement avec votre serviteur." A reference to Psalm 143:2.</note>

How holy and how equitable is your judgment!
    I have delivered myself into your hands,
    Divine Master of my destinies:
    I can no longer be held accountable.
You possess my goods, I have surrendered everything to you,
    I would not know how to give you an account,
Love is my Guarantor, & you have permitted me
    To present it to you without shame.
Alas, if you wished to settle accounts with me,
    I would soon be reduced to dust;
    My spirit, filled entirely with terror,
    Would wait trembling for your lightning bolt.
        To avoid this great misfortune
    I have left this wretched self behind;
    I have surrendered everything to you, Lord,
    Remaining in an extreme state of nothingness. <note>The author refers to "le néant" (nothingness), a central concept in 17th-century mysticism involving the total annihilation of the ego to unite with God.</note>
I never kept count, O my Sovereign Good,
    Of either labors or of suffering:
    If I remain within my nothingness
Can you exercise your vengeance upon me?
Without counting, I am willing to undergo the august law
    Of Justice which is dear to me:
    But I do not see, O my King,
    Where your anger would fall:
    The lightning strikes against solid bodies:
    I cannot fear its efforts;
For upon nothingness, it can do nothing.
My divine Master, alas, on that terrible day, <note>A reference to the Day of Judgment.</note>
    Judge me only on the basis of love.

<meta>catchword: XI. That</meta>

<summary>A poetic meditation on Psalm 143:2, where the soul pleads for mercy by claiming a state of total self-annihilation and "nothingness," arguing that Divine wrath has no target when the ego has been surrendered.</summary>
<keywords>Herman Hugo, Divine Judgment, surrender of self, mysticism, nothingness, Divine Justice, Love as guarantor</keywords>

# E M B L E M S

### XI.

->*Let the storm not submerge me; and let me*<-
->*not be buried in this abyss.*<-
<note>This is a paraphrase of Psalm 69:15, a common theme in 17th-century mystical literature representing the "dark night" or overwhelming spiritual trials.</note>

I am almost swallowed up by the storm and the waves,
I see a horrible tempest breaking over me;
The thunderbolt already above my head
Robs me of hope and rest.
Come to my aid, sole Author of my flame <note>The "flame" refers to the soul's divine love or "sacred fire."</note>,
Without you, without you I shall perish:
See the turmoil of my soul;
Alas! deign to succor me.

Ah, it is not in vain, Great God, that one calls upon you;
You come at my piercing cries;
And in the most pressing dangers,
How faithful your love appears!
I was almost engulfed in the depths of the sea,
I was sinking ever deeper into the wave;
But your grace, without equal,
Withdrew me just as I was about to be destroyed.

->*Our Lord.*<-

I pull you from here for a greater shipwreck;
I wish to immerse you in love:
It is there that you will find one day
Both your loss and your gain. <note>A reference to the mystical paradox where losing one's self-will leads to finding divine life.</note>

->*The Soul.*<-

Only draw me out from the state where I am,
O you, Lord, in whom I hope.
My heart is too smitten with your will
To not wish to satisfy you in everything.
Do, do with me according to your pleasure,
Deign to give me constancy;
I shall no longer fear suffering,
I already feel for it a sovereign desire.

<meta>catchword: XII. Who</meta>

<summary>This emblem depicts the soul's transition from the terrifying "storm" of worldly or spiritual affliction to a voluntary "shipwreck" in divine love. It emphasizes the paradox that true spiritual gain is found through the total surrender of the self to God's will.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual storm, divine grace, shipwreck of love, surrender, constancy, mystical union, Psalm 69, salvation</keywords>

# FROM H. HUGO.

## XII.

> *Who will be able to grant me this grace, that you might place me under cover, and hide me in hell, until your fury has entirely passed?* <note>This is a translation of the Latin Vulgate version of Job 14:13, a common theme in penitential literature where the speaker seeks refuge even in the grave to escape immediate wrath.</note>
>
> What shall I do, Lord, to avoid your blows,
> ->To hide myself from your anger?<-
> ->Is there some cavern beneath the earth<-
> Where I might be sheltered from your righteous wrath?
>
> ->I am pierced with grief<-
> ->For having drawn out your vengeance;<-
> ->I yield much less to fear<-
> ->Than to the displeasure of my offense.<- <note>The author distinguishes between "attrition" (fear of punishment) and "contrition" (sorrow for having offended God).</note>
>
> Alas, if you wished to punish me today
> ->By making your anger cease,<-
> ->I would see my boredom change,<- <note>In this context, "ennui" refers to a deep spiritual distress or agony rather than mere boredom.</note>
> Ah Lord, in whom alone I hope!
>
> ->The pain of having displeased you<-
> ->Gives me a cruel suffering,<-
> ->My heart ceases to be rebellious,<-
> Beneath the force of your blows it finds itself beaten down.
>
> Do not abandon me to my own misery,
> ->O you, you, Savior of mankind;<-
> Suspend for some time your severe justice,
> Deign to protect me with your powerful hands.
>
> ->I know that your mercies<-
> ->Surpass our iniquity:<-
> If I obtain my pardon, and if you grant it to me
> I will satisfy you through my humility.

<summary>A poetic meditation based on Job 14:13, where the speaker expresses deep contrition and begs for God's mercy and protection from divine wrath. The poem emphasizes that the speaker's sorrow stems more from the pain of offending God than from the fear of punishment.</summary>

# E M B L E M S

### XIII.

> *Will the few days that remain to me not soon come to an end? Grant me then a little respite, so that I may breathe in the midst of my sorrow.* <note>This is a paraphrase of Job 10:20, a common scriptural reference in 17th-century devotional literature.</note>

Leave me to weep for my sorrow,
Sweet architect of my martyrdom. <note>original: "martire" - here used in the mystical sense of suffering for love of God.</note>
O you, for whom my heart sighs,
How quickly you have changed your fury!

Hardly have I wept for my offense for a time,
Than you come to bring me relief:
Let my days flow away in penance,
You know very poorly how to take your revenge.

I am near my end, and my days like a shadow
Will vanish in an instant:
Ah, in this somber dwelling
Leave me to weep, dear Lover. <note>original: "Amant" - in mystical poetry, Christ is frequently addressed as the Lover or Bridegroom of the soul.</note>

You wish for me to console myself
Even after having displeased you,
And your divine word
Is going to make me forget all that is owed to you.

Your caresses full of charms
Even despite my heart have made my tears dry up;
I already feel peace flooding my spirit:
And I no longer experience those cruel alarms
Which used to leave me utterly speechless.

Since you wish it, I abandon my soul
To this divine calm that your Lovers taste,
I feel that flame being born within me
Which creates all their contentment.

Do not suffer, Lord, that my heart should offend
you,
Prevent my crime by punishing my sin:
I will adore this vengeance
If my heart is not stained by infidelity.

<meta>catchword: XIV. Ah</meta>

<summary>This emblem depicts the soul's transition from a state of penitent grief to divine consolation, where the speaker finds God's mercy so overwhelming that it prevents the full expression of penance. It explores the mystical paradox of "sweet martyrdom" and the overwhelming peace of divine love.</summary>
<keywords>divine love, penance, consolation, mysticism, Job 10:20, spiritual peace, repentance</keywords>

## XIV.

->Ah, if only they had wisdom! Ah, if they understood<-
->my conduct, and if they foresaw how<-
->everything will end!<-

You show me, Lord, this future glory,
In order to console my heart:
This greatly pleases my human nature;
But I wish to love you with much more fervor.

Hide this reward from me,
Which you keep for your children:
Let me love you with that constancy
Which expects nothing from your gifts.

Even if you had nothing to give me
But the flames <note>The fires of Hell or Purgatory</note> for my portion,
I would still want to love you
And serve you with the same courage.

But could I possess it if you do not give it—
This pure love <term>Pure Love: a theological concept of loving God for His own sake, without hope of reward or fear of punishment</term> that I desire?
It is an effect of your goodness;
I would wish to buy it through a harsh martyrdom.

In order to acquire it, I have nothing to give,
For I am poverty itself:
I can, in everything, abandon myself,
And show you thereby, Great God, that I love you.

Receive my nothingness; it is my only possession:
Nothingness is my only portion.
I want You, or I want nothing;
Be, O Love, my only inheritance!

<sig>X V. My</sig>

<vocab>Lord, future glory, pure love, poverty, nothingness, Love, inheritance, Herman Hugo</vocab>

<summary>A poetic meditation on "Pure Love," where the speaker asks God to hide the promise of Heaven so they may love Him without seeking reward. The soul offers its own "nothingness" as the only gift it can return to the Creator.</summary>
<keywords>Herman Hugo, Pure Love, mysticism, disinterested love, poverty of spirit, divine inheritance, martyrdom</keywords>

# E M B L E M S

### X V.

*My life is consumed with grief, and my years are spent in groaning.* <note>A paraphrase of Psalm 31:10.</note>

My days have passed in groaning,
->In sorrows my life flows away;<-
->But, O King of all Lovers,<-
->I shall soon be set free from them.<-

I see death from afar, which seems to approach me;
->I dare not show any joy:<-
->I fear to displease you.<-
->Alas, grant that I may see you!<-

You can, all at once, purify my heart,
->And make it so conformed to yours,<-
->Despite this weak languor,<-
->That nothing of the human remains within it.<- <note>The author refers to the "old man" or the earthly self that must die to allow for divine union.</note>

That, freed from everything, I may no longer exist:
->Tear me away, my Lord, from myself:<-
->That I may live only in JESUS;<-
->And that He alone within me may adore and love Himself!<-

Ah, I am reduced to nothingness:
->His love has ravished from me that former vigor,<-
->Which made me run incessantly<-
->Toward that source of light.<-

I can no longer act; I can only suffer;
->My heart itself, my heart, can no longer groan:<-
->It experiences a profound peace,<-
->As if it were alone in the world.<-

I no longer know myself, I know not if I am,
->I have neither strength nor power;<-
->Your arms, which serve as my support,<-
->Do not take away my fainting weakness.<-

I am unable to will, I have no more desire,
->My soul is dead to all things;<-
->Is it not time, dear Spouse, to die,<- <note>The "Spouse" is a common mystical term for Christ, reflecting the Song of Solomon.</note>
And to reunite me to the First Cause? <note>The "First Cause" is a philosophical and theological term for God as the origin of all existence.</note>

## X V I.

->*My soul has longed with great ardor for your*<-
->*ordinances.*<- <note>original: "ordonnances." This refers to the divine laws or decrees of God, often echoing Psalm 119.</note>

Retire, begone, deceitful love,
->I detest you and I abhor you.<-
Since the time that I gave my heart
To this sovereign God whom I love and I adore,
I have no longer listened to your profane discourse:
->Do you truly dare to come again,<-
In order to disturb me in my chaste loves?

He who holds my heart will know well how to defend it.
->Leave behind your bow and your blindfold,<- <note>A reference to Cupid, the classical personification of earthly desire.</note>
->Or retire to a new country:<-
The flames of love which have reduced me to ash
Mean that I can taste nothing here below:
->When one has known His charms,<-
Can one still let oneself be captured by a vain object?

->O my celestial Spouse,<- <note>term: Celestial Spouse (Epoux céleste). A common mystical metaphor where the soul is the bride and Christ is the bridegroom.</note>
My eyes, my chaste eyes see nothing but you:
All other objects are funereal objects
Which would make me perish in the midst of darkness.
You are my happiness, you are my light;
I know only you, sovereign Beauty.
It is you who penetrate the center of my soul,
It is you who burn me with such a sweet flame,
->That I never wish to be cured of it:<-
Burn my heart always, or make me die!

-># EMBLEMS<-

### XVII.

*Deign, O Lord, to direct my paths in such a way, that I may keep the justice of your ordinances.* <note>This is a paraphrase of Psalm 119:5.</note>

In this terrible labyrinth,
So full of twists and turns,
I walk, dear Spouse <note>The "Spouse" refers to Christ, following the tradition of mystical marriage between the soul and God.</note>, without fear
Relying on the faith of your help.

I watch from afar as they fall into the precipice,
Even the boldest and the most clear-sighted:
I go without seeing, and all my artifice
Is to abandon myself to the care of my Lover. <note>original: "Amant" — in 17th-century French spirituality, this term denotes a deep, divine affection rather than a secular romance.</note>

This blind man is a great example
Of abandonment and of faith;
When I contemplate him from afar
I feel myself ravished out of myself.

He follows his little dog and walks with assurance
Without stumbling or making a false step.
I am guided by your providence
And could I fail to abandon myself to you?

He who counts upon his own strength,
Upon his skill and his agility,
With his pride serving as his bait,
Is immediately cast down.

Who can, in such great danger,
Still trust in themselves;
Ah, how extreme is their audacity!
You taught me to place myself
Under the care of providence,
And this admirable science <note>term: science — here meaning a spiritual discipline or "holy knowledge" rather than modern laboratory science.</note>
Left me with nothing more to manage for myself.

This life is a labyrinth;
If one wishes to walk surely,
Let our faith be blind and without feint,
Our love pure, and without disguise.

<column-break/>

| **Spouse** | **Lover** |
| **faith** | **science** |
| **assurance** | **disguise** |

<summary>This emblem uses the metaphor of a labyrinth and a blind man led by a dog to illustrate the soul's need for total surrender to Divine Providence. It argues that human intellect and pride lead to spiritual ruin, while "blind" faith leads to safety.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Providence, spiritual labyrinth, mysticism, abandonment to God, faith vs. reason, mystical marriage</keywords>

### X V I I I .

> ->*Strengthen my steps in your paths, so that my*<-
> ->*feet may not be shaken.*<-
<note>original: "Afermiſſez mes pas dans vos ſentiers, afin que mes pieds ne ſoient point ébranlés." This is a translation of Psalm 17:5.</note>

I am but a child, I do not know how to walk,
Divine Love, ah, lead me yourself!
May my weakness, O God, touch you one day:
How great it is, and how extreme!
You teach me the true paths
Which lead to righteousness:
Without your powerful hand I see only mires;
Then abysses, and precipices.
I tremble at every step; ah, come to my aid!
This support serves me but little;
Without the sustenance of my beloved
I may at any moment turn back.
Love, do not abandon me;
Always rule and guide my steps.

<summary>A poetic prayer based on Psalm 17, where the soul confesses its childlike frailty and begs Divine Love to guide its steps away from the spiritual dangers of the world.</summary>
<keywords>Herman Hugo, Divine Love, spiritual guidance, Psalm 17, righteousness, humility, pilgrimage</keywords>

# EMBLEMS

## XIX.

> ->*Pierce my flesh with your fear: for I am seized with terror at the sight of your judgments.* <note>original: "Percez ma chair de votre crainte : car je suis saisi de fraieur dans la vûe de vos jugemens." This is a translation of Psalm 119:120.</note><-

Lord, a vile speck of dust,
A nothingness full of vanity,
Unworthy of your anger,
Should instead draw out your kindness.

No, no, it is not your blows,
Divine Love, that I apprehend;
I fear only your wrath:
Alas, how great is my sorrow!

Where can I go to hide myself?
My terror increases without ceasing;
For avenging justice
Will surely reach me without even searching.

I see, however, my dear Master,
That beneath this mask of fury
You wish to hide yourself, perhaps;
My harm will not be as great as my fear.

Alas, I am such a small thing!
Do you wish to destroy me in an instant?
You, my principle and my first cause,
Can reduce me to nothingness.

Ah, withdraw then your lightning;
There is no need for your darts.
In order to reduce me to powder,
It takes only one of your glances.

<meta>XX. De-</meta>

<summary>Emblem XIX presents a poetic meditation on Psalm 119, where the speaker expresses profound humility and fear of God's judgment while hoping for divine mercy. The text reflects the "Theology of the Cross" common in 17th-century devotion, emphasizing the soul's insignificance compared to the Creator.</summary>
<keywords>divine justice, humility, fear of God, nothingness, mercy, spiritual emblems, 17th-century devotion</keywords>

## XX.

->*Turn away my eyes, so that they do not look*<-
->*upon vanity.*<- <note>A translation of Psalm 119:37, a common theme in 17th-century devotional literature regarding the "distraction of the senses."</note>

All the pleasures that are esteemed in this world,
Flow away faster than a wave;
Happy are those who turn their eyes away
From this flattering world, despising its language,
They shall have a double advantage;
Their spirit delivered from odious objects,
They can contemplate the Monarch of the heavens.

It is you, divine Love, who works these wonders:
As soon as one abandons oneself to you
You guard us from the world and its blows,
And overwhelm us with unparalleled graces.

You make us hate foolish vanity,
And make us love august Truth,
You lead our steps according to your wisdom
Making us avoid a dull <term>softness</term> <note>The original "mollesse" refers to a state of spiritual lethargy or moral weakness often associated with luxury.</note>.

Ah, hide me always from this deceptive object!
This fine and cunning <term>seducer</term> <note>The "suborneur" or seducer represents the world or the devil attempting to lead the soul astray.</note>
Enchants with his false pleasures,
And could carry away the heart of your Beloved.

# E M B L E M S

### XXI.

> *Grant that my heart may keep itself pure in the prac-*
> *tice of your ordinances full of justice; so*
> *that I may not be put to shame.* <note>A paraphrase of Psalm 119:80: "Let my heart be sound in thy statutes; that I be not ashamed."</note>

->I shall never misuse it,<-
->Governing myself by what pleases you.<-
Let your holy law be renewed within me,
And, without straying from your divine paths,
->May my heart be always in your hands:<-
->Lead it, Supreme Goodness:<-
->Do more, lose it within yourself.<- <note>The author refers to the mystical concept of "holy indifference" or the annihilation of the self in God.</note>

->May it be entirely hidden from my sight,<-
->Engulfed in the naked Essence;<- <term>Naked Essence: a mystical term referring to the pure, unmediated nature of God, stripped of all human concepts or images.</term>
I shall forever bless its most happy destiny.

# XXII.

->*Come, my beloved, let us go forth into the fields,*<-
->*let us abide in the villages.*<-
<note>original: "Veni, dilecte mi, egrediamur in agrum, commoremur in villis." A quote from the Song of Solomon 7:11, a central text for mystical poetry.</note>

Come, my dear Spouse, let us dwell in the village,
Let us leave the city and its encumbrances,
I wish to follow your steps everywhere;
I would rather inhabit some wild cave.
There, far from the world and its noise
I wish to love you and speak to you without ceasing,
I shall have the calm of the night:
There I will contemplate your divine wisdom.
Walking with you I cannot grow weary,
You give new strength:
Following these eternal routes
One walks day and night, even without thinking of it.
Let us depart right now, my adorable Master,
Without ever turning back on our steps:
Ah, I would perhaps lose my way,
Divine Love, if I did not have you:
What am I saying? It would undoubtedly be true.
If you left me for a single moment
Oh, how great would be my ruin,
If I were not guided by my faithful Lover!
<note>The term "Lover" (Amant) here refers to the "Divine Lover" or Christ, a common metaphor in 17th-century devotional literature representing the relationship between the soul and God.</note>

<meta>catchword: XXIII. Ti-</meta>

<summary>This page features a poetic meditation on Song of Solomon 7:11, where the soul entreats the Divine Spouse to leave the distractions of the city for the solitude of the countryside. It emphasizes the soul's total dependence on God's guidance to avoid losing its way.</summary>
<keywords>Herman Hugo, Song of Solomon, Divine Spouse, solitude, mystical union, spiritual journey, 17th-century devotion</keywords>

# E M B L E M S

## XXIII.

->*Draw me: we will run after you at the scent of your perfumes.* <note>This is a translation of the Latin Vulgate from the Song of Solomon 1:4: "Trahe me: post te curremus in odorem unguentorum tuorum."</note><-

Draw me, my divine Spouse;
Then we shall run after you:
For the sweet scent of your heavenly perfumes,
By drawing me out of my fatal lethargy, <note>The original "langueurs funestes" refers to a state of spiritual dullness or depression.</note>
Revives me and ravishes my senses:
This fragrance, sweeter than incense,
Invites me ceaselessly to follow you:
Without this divine perfume, I could no longer live.

How much of a novice you still are in your love,
The Spouse replied in his turn:
You desire the attractive sweetness of perfumes;
You are a weak lover!

I know a path more solid and more short;
It is that of my pure love.
There, one seeks neither perfume nor tenderness;
One is guided by Wisdom: <term>Sagesse (Wisdom)</term> <note>In this mystical context, Wisdom refers to the Sophia or the divine intellect that guides the soul beyond mere emotion.</note>

It is there that grief, pain, and torment
Distinguish the perfect Lover.
What! do you wish to walk upon the blooming rose
When I have seen my life end in torments?
Follow me into hardships, expire upon the cross:
Then you shall be worthy of my choice.

<meta>catchword: XXIV. Who</meta>

<summary>Emblem XXIII presents a dialogue based on the Song of Solomon, where the Soul asks to be drawn by divine sweetness, but the Spouse corrects her, stating that true love is proven through suffering and the Cross rather than sensory delights.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Solomon, Divine Spouse, mystical marriage, spiritual perfumes, suffering, the Cross, pure love, Wisdom</keywords>

### XXIV.

->*Who will give you to me, O my brother, sucking the*<-

->I no longer fear that I will be despised:<-
->For I wish to lead him outside;<-

<note>The term "transports" (raptures) refers to an ecstatic state of spiritual joy.</note>
Divine child, author of my long suffering,
->You revive my hope;<-
I find you now; what an excess of pleasure!
->I will set my desire before you;<-
It is to see myself united with you without division:
->Grant me this advantage,<-
->Then I shall fear nothing more,<-
Peaceful possessor of my only good.

# EMBLEMS

### XXV.

> *I sought him whom my soul loves in my little bed during the nights: I sought him; and I found him not.* <note>A translation of Song of Solomon 3:1. The author uses the "little bed" as a metaphor for spiritual laziness or worldly comfort.</note>

Why do you seek in bed
->Your Spouse, indiscreet Lover?<- <note>The "Spouse" refers to Christ, and the "Lover" to the human soul, a common theme in mystical poetry.</note>
In vain you seek him there in this dark night;
->He does not make his retreat there.<-

Step forward a little, there he is upon the Cross
->Pierced with nails, adorned with thorns;<-
You will never find him except upon this wood,
Sorrows and pains are his divine paths.

It is quite in vain that we seek
Jesus in the repose of an unworthy softness: <term>mollesse (softness)</term> <note>In this context, it refers to a life of ease, luxury, or spiritual apathy.</note>
->Never shall we find him there;<-
He lives in sorrow, he dies in sadness:
->He tires himself incessantly<-
->To win over the sinful soul;<-
->His rest is found in torment.<-

Let us suffer, let us die to everything; we shall find without
->difficulty,<-
->The illustrious Spouse of our heart.<-
->It is a very vain search<-
To wish to find our Savior in bed.

<meta>catchword: XXVI. I</meta>

<summary>Emblem XXV reflects on Song of Solomon 3:1, arguing that Christ (the Spouse) cannot be found in a life of comfort or "softness," but only through the suffering of the Cross. It encourages the soul to embrace pain and self-denial to achieve true union with the Savior.</summary>
<keywords>Emblems, Christ as Spouse, The Cross, Suffering, Spiritual Discipline, Song of Solomon, Mysticism, Repentance</keywords>

# OF H. HUGO.

### X X V I.

> *I will rise now, and go about the city in the streets, and in the broad ways I will seek him whom my soul loveth: I sought him, but I found him not.* <note>This is a translation of the Latin Vulgate text of Song of Solomon 3:2.</note>

No, no, I no longer wish to live in repose,
I want to run everywhere seeking the one I love:
I have sought him inopportunely,
Never again will I do the same.
Of a great city I shall make the circuit
To bear witness to him of my love.

->What are you doing, O foolish Lover?<-

Ah, how poorly you seek, always at the wrong time!
You follow nothing but your own inclination,
And let yourself be guided by the senses.
You seek in the bed; Jesus is on the Cross:
He is near to you; yet you run through the city.
You are mistaken in your choice:
Do not leave this little dwelling <note>The "little domicile" refers to the interior of the heart or the cell of contemplation.</note>.
Love, suffer for him, who will take your heart,
In order to make it his retreat:
Then you will be satisfied,
At all times, in all places possessing this happiness.
You will taste peace even in suffering,
You will desire nothing more;
And your heart, content to possess this good,
You will have all the rest in superabundance.

### X X V I I.

> *Have you not seen him whom my soul loves?* <note>A reference to Song of Solomon 3:3-4.</note>
> *When I had passed a little beyond them,*
> *I found him whom my soul loves: I hold him;*
> *and I will let him go no more.*

BY distancing myself from every creature <note>In mystical theology, "creature" refers to any created thing or person other than God.</note>
I have found my celestial Spouse:
When I followed my nature too closely
I deprived myself of a blessing so sweet.

I hold him, this faithful Lover,
I will no longer suffer him to stray from me;
I swear to him today an eternal love
And forever an inviolable faith.

Let us remain, dear Spouse, in this solitude,
I will reveal my fires <note>The "fires" of spiritual passion or divine love.</note> to you:
I will suffer no dark anxiety there:
To possess you is the goal of my vows.

There, separated and far from all things
I will tell you of my loves:
Ah, grant that my heart may rest in your heart,
And that it may rest there always!

# O F H. H U G O.

## X X V I I I.

> *->But for me, all my good is to keep myself united<-*
> *->to God, and to place all my hope<-*
> *->in the Lord, my God.<-* <note>original: "Mais pour moi, tout mon bien est de me tenir uni à Dieu..." This is a paraphrase of Psalm 73:28.</note>

How good it is for me to cling to you,
And to place my confidence there!
Is there anything, my divine Spouse, <note>The "Spouse" refers to Christ, following the tradition of the Song of Solomon where the soul is the bride of God.</note>
More charming than this adherence?
There our hearts are united, we have but one will,
My hope is not in vain;
I experience the divine power,
Which is pleased to carry me with a sovereign hand.
I no longer fear pain or danger,
Carried as I am by this God whom I adore.
How light the torment appears!
I love Him all the more, O pleasure, as I abhor you.
What a change, great God, I discover in my heart!
I used to love vanity, now I see it as detestable:
I used to fear the slightest pain;
Now torment appears lovely to me.
It is you, divine Love, who have done this good for me,
For without you I could do nothing.

# E M B L E M S

### XXIX.

->*I rested under the shadow of him whom I had so greatly desired.*<-
<note>This is a paraphrase of Song of Solomon 2:3: "I sat down under his shadow with great delight."</note>

Alas, what pains and labors I have suffered!
I was wandering and a vagabond,
I found nothing in the world
That could serve to soothe my ills.
Happily, I have found upon this wood
<note>The "wood" refers to the Cross of Christ, a common motif in 17th-century mystical poetry.</note>
The one whom my soul loves:
By an extreme happiness,
My heart has made this worthy choice.
I have found my rest beneath this fertile tree,
Where love holds him bound;
I have chosen it for my dwelling,
My heart can no longer be detached from it.
I rest beneath his shadow,
It is where I live both night and day:
The more my dwelling appears somber,
The more it has what is needed to please my Love.
There I find fruits of an exquisite sweetness;
Others would find them bitter:
For me, I confess with frankness
That I have seen none such in the universe.

<meta>catchword: XXX. Com-</meta>

<summary>In this emblem, the soul reflects on its transition from a weary wanderer to finding eternal rest and spiritual nourishment beneath the "shadow" of the Beloved on the Cross. It emphasizes that the hardships of the divine life, though appearing bitter or dark to the world, are sweet to the devoted heart.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual rest, the Cross, Song of Solomon, divine love, mystical union, wandering soul, spiritual fruit</keywords>

### XXX.

->*How could we sing the songs of the*<-
->*Lord in a foreign land?*<-
<note>original: "Quomodo cantabimus canticum Domini in terra aliena?" from Psalm 137:4. This refers to the Babylonian Captivity, used here as a metaphor for the soul's exile from Heaven.</note>

->*The Soul.*<-

How could I, alas, in this foreign land
->Still strike up holy melodies?<-
When I was near you, my Lord and my
->Father,<-
I composed sacred concerts.
->Now I leave my lyre behind,<-
I can no longer strike up songs:
->I must, I must sigh;<-
My sad heart has no more music left.

->*Our Lord.*<-

It is I, it is I, who wishes that for my
->glory<-
You may sing in every place:
For there is no dwelling-place so dark,
Where one ought not to love and burn with my fires.

->*The Soul.*<-

Let us sing then, dear Spouse: how beautiful the harmony is
->When two hearts are truly in love,<-
And their flame is chaste and faithful,
How marvelous is this accord!
->It is a concert that is always the same,<-
One finds no false notes there,
->Never does one perceive a "No":<-
What one desires, when love is extreme,
->The other answers at that very instant:<-
Never a different note:
->Oh, how charming is this Canticle,<-
Which denotes divine Love!
Let us sing, my heart, both night and day:
One cannot sing too much when one is full of love.

<summary>A poetic dialogue between the Soul and Christ based on Psalm 137, where the Soul's initial mourning in earthly exile is transformed into a harmonious song of divine love. The text emphasizes the perfect mystical union between the believer and God, characterized by a "concert" without discord.</summary>

# BOOK III.

## XXXI.

> *I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem, if you*
> *find my Beloved, tell him that*
> *I languish with love.* <note>A direct reference to the Song of Solomon 5:8, a common source for mystical emblems.</note>

O You, whom I perceive, my faithful Companions,
->You who travel through the countryside,<-
->If you should meet one day<-
My Spouse <note>original: "Epoux"; representing Christ or the Divine</note>, tell him that I languish with love.

->Alas! I have run just as you have<-
->To meet the one I love:<-
->All my labors seemed sweet to me<-
->In order to find this lovely Spouse:<-
But at present my weakness is extreme.
My heart is pierced by his divine charms,
->And I no longer know how to take a step,<-
I find my rest in the love that enchants me;
->And this rest reduces me to the last gasp.<-
Alas! I would cease to be so listless
If I could hear once more his adorable voice.

->Tell him that I am dying;<-
Paint for him my torment, O my lovely sisters:
->Inform him that his Beloved,<- <note>original: "Amante"; the soul personified as a female lover</note>
Is ready to expire under the weight of her sorrows.

### XXXII.

->*Sustain me with flowers, strengthen me with apples: because I languish with love.*<- <note>original: "Soutenez moi avec des fleurs, fortifiez moi avec des pommes : parce que je languis d'amour." This is a translation of Song of Solomon 2:5.</note>

Alas, I am dying! Ah, cover me with flowers,
        Do not abandon me, my sisters,
        Surround me with those apples
        Found in the garden of the Bridegroom:
        Ah, hide me from all men;
        And let me be alone with you.

"Of what use, incomparable Lover,
"Are these apples and these flowers? You are languishing;
"You require the celestial favors of love:
        "Do you fear a lack of flowers?
                "It is no longer these trifles <term>bagatelles: small, insignificant things</term>
                "Which must now bring you relief:
"Thorns and crosses, these are the new flowers
        "Which the Bridegroom wishes to share with you.
                "Leave the savory apple behind;
                "You must become generous <note>In this context, "generous" implies a nobility of spirit and a willingness to suffer for God.</note>
        "If you wish to please the celestial Bridegroom;
"This is the means to draw him into you."

# EMBLEMS

### XXXIII.

> *My Beloved is mine, and I am his. He feeds among the lilies, until the day begins to break, and the shadows gradually flee away.* <note>This is a translation of Song of Solomon 2:16-17, a central text in Christian mysticism representing the soul's union with God.</note>

It is finished, it is finished; I desire flowers no more,
->Unless it be to weave a crown<-
For my celestial Spouse; and for him I abandon
->From this moment on such tasteless sweets.<-

->The gathering of lilies surrounding me<-
->Represents my purity;<-
->And it is my Spouse who bestows it;<-
Whatever is not from him is nothing but vanity. <note>original: "vanité" — in this context, meaning emptiness or lack of spiritual substance.</note>

Dear and divine Spouse, ah, keep your favors;
What you give to me, I wish to return at once:
->These flowers are not enough,<-
My heart is entirely yours, never to be taken back.

->We shall rejoice in the midst of these lilies,<-
->Until the day returns;<-
->Delightful are my nights,<-
When you permit me then to converse with you!

If I am entirely yours, you are entirely mine,
->My happiness, my joy is extreme,<-
->Love is my only law;<-
You love me: You know, Lord, that I love you.

<term>Emblem</term> (A symbolic picture accompanied by a motto and a poetic reflection), <term>Spouse</term> (In mystical tradition, Christ is the Bridegroom and the human soul is the Bride), <term>Lilies</term> (Symbols of purity and the presence of the Divine), <term>Vanity</term> (The fleeting nature of worldly things), <term>Love</term>, <term>Purity</term>, <term>Lord</term>

<summary>This page presents the 33rd emblem, a poetic meditation on the Song of Solomon emphasizing the soul's total surrender and exclusive love for the divine Spouse. It uses the imagery of lilies to represent a purity that is gifted by God rather than earned by the self.</summary>

### XXXIV.

->*I am my Beloved’s, and his heart turns toward me.* <note>A paraphrase of Song of Solomon 7:10: "I am my beloved's, and his desire is toward me."</note><-

My heart follows you everywhere, O my divine Lover,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Just as iron follows its magnet:
You mark upon my heart, as if upon a compass,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;By your glances, by your word,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Your adorable will,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And you turn me in every direction.

The Heliotrope <note>The sunflower or "turn-sole," a common symbol in mystical literature for the soul that always turns toward God, the Sun of Justice.</note> also turns toward the light
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Of its Sun, with whom it is in love;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And being unable to leave the earth,
It wishes, like the sun, to make the circuit of the heavens.

My heart, thus converted toward love,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Finds that love is its living light:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It guides me in my career <note>In this context, "career" refers to one's path or course through life.</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It creates both my night and my day.

If he moves away from me, I am in darkness;
When he is near me, the night becomes brightness:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He inspires me with his truth;
Without him, all objects are but mournful things.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Without him, I would be in death;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He is within me the spirit and the life;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;From all evils I am set free
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Without making any effort of my own.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;**HE IS MINE, I AM HIS;**
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;How reciprocal is this love!
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Nothing in this is ambiguous
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Since he is its firm support.

<summary>This poem uses the metaphors of a compass needle and a heliotrope flower to describe the soul's total orientation toward the Divine. It emphasizes a mystical reciprocity where the soul finds life and light only through the presence of the Beloved.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, Song of Solomon, Heliotrope, Compass, Mysticism, Herman Hugo, Soul's Journey</keywords>
