# X L.

-> *He who wishes to love is no longer free in his own way.* <-

How I love your yoke, how sweet and pleasant it is,
How vainly I used to fear it!
I am free, far from being a slave,
When I bear it while loving you.
How happy my soul is, being your captive!
I have found my liberty there.
Grant then, O Love <note>The author uses "Amour" (Love) as a name for God or the Divine Presence</note>, that I may live
In humble dependence upon your will.

O happy yoke, which far from imprisoning my soul,
Causes a vast delight,
How well you accord with the sweet flame
That I keep in my heart like a precious gift!

The world, which sees only the apparent burden
By which I seem overwhelmed in its eyes,
Believes me very miserable: but my heart is at 
liberty;
Far from being a slave, it is filled with delights.

No, the world hardly understands
Despite so many labors, the happiness within;
Valuing only that which prospers,
Honors, pleasures, and that which flatters the senses.

The children of JESUS have much more wisdom,
Valuing nothing, tasting only the cross:
Ah, what delicacy their taste possesses,
To know how to make such a good choice!

I yield to you, worldly ones, the honors and delights;
I love all my labors, my chain, my prison:
Even if I had to suffer every torment,
I would still find that I have great reason:
Let us say without artifice,
That he who knows Love and its true value,
And who knows how to do it justice,
Will approve of the inclination of my heart.

<meta>catchword: XLI. The Uni-</meta>

<summary>Emblem XL explores the spiritual paradox where submitting to the "yoke" of divine love results in true freedom rather than slavery. The text contrasts the internal joy of the believer with the world's superficial perception of religious life as a burden.</summary>

<keywords>divine love, spiritual liberty, the yoke of Christ, internal happiness, worldly vanity, devotion, mysticism</keywords>

# DO. VÆNIUS. <note>Latin: Dominicus Vænius, the Latinized name of the artist Otto van Veen.</note>

### X L I.

->*The Unique Love shines among the virtues.*<-

Love, divine Love, who encompasses within yourself
The supreme excellence of all the virtues,
Source of justice and support of faith,
Everything that one hopes for is enclosed within you.

Without you, penance is a hypocrisy,
Prudence and strength a pure mania;
Without you, divine Love, crosses, martyrdoms, and torments,
Would be but vain amusements.

It is therefore sacred Love that regulates all things;
It is the goal proposed to us,
It gives to all goods their price and value,
Everything would be languishing without its noble vigor.
It makes that which crawled upon the earth fly to heaven,
It brings into our hearts both peace and war;
It is always through its care that one is victorious:
It rights our steps, it opens our eyes.

How unhappy one would be without its sweet assistance!
It is in our labors our unique hope,
In our afflictions it is our recourse.
Sacred Love, rule and conduct my days
By the order of your Providence;
I wish to live and die under your dependence!

# E M B L E M S.

## XLII.

->*Love overcomes all.*<-

Who can resist Love?
He who overcomes everything, whose invincible strength
Despite forts and ramparts, pierces, breaks, and makes a way,
Reaching that which appears most inaccessible.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;God yields to our strong ardor,
He suspends his wrath, is appeased and lays down his arms
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;When he discovers in the depths of our heart
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;That Love is the source of our tears.
Love, powerful Love and sovereign victor,
How charming are your blows! How I love your wounds!
Shoot, cut, destroy, do not spare my breast,
Make, make my blood flow through a hundred thousand openings,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Leave nothing that is not entirely divine,
Remove impurity, cleanse the filth,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Banish what remains of the human,
You desire for your children souls that are entirely pure.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You only destroy a heart to make it strong:
When it no longer belongs to itself, God moves and animates it;
He achieves everything without making any effort:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;This figure <note>Referring to the engraving that would have accompanied this emblem in the original printed book.</note> expresses to us
How divine Love guides the bow and the arm
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Of this fortunate Lover;
See how skillfully and without any embarrassment
She shoots her arrow to conquer destiny:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;She pierces with the first blow
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;This thick and strong breastplate:
No, there is nothing that cannot be accomplished
Aided by Love, for its strength surpasses
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The harshest effort of Hell,
Finally, Love is stronger than death. <note>A reference to the Song of Solomon 8:6, "For love is strong as death."</note>

XLIII. Acti-

<summary>This emblem illustrates the omnipotence of Divine Love, depicting it as an archer capable of piercing any defense and purifying the soul through sacred suffering. It concludes with the theological triumph of love over both hell and death.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, spiritual purification, mysticism, spiritual warfare, victory over death, sacred wounds</keywords>

# XLIII.

->Agitated, it becomes firmer.<-

The more I am agitated, the more strength I feel;
   The storm serves only to better steady me;
Since my dear Spouse <note>In mystical tradition, the "Spouse" refers to Christ or the Divine, following the imagery of the Song of Solomon.</note> deigns to sustain me,
   Evils touch only the surface.
The more afflictions I have, the more I experience within
A sense of peace and sweetness: Sovereign Goodness,
   In exchange for a mere appearance of pain,
   Overwhelms me with contentment.
Come and strike me, all the arrows of envy,
   I laugh at your vain efforts:
      The most painful life
      And the harshest deaths,
Are an infinite source of infinite blessings,
   And through the storm, one is led to the harbor.
   Ah, how ravished a soul is then!
   How it then blesses its fate!
      God repays with interest
      A brief sorrow,
      Giving Himself without measure
To whoever, for His sake, despises a brief and vain happiness.
Holy sweetness of heaven, pleasant ideas,
You fill the heart that wishes to receive you;
By your powerful attractions, possessed souls
   Do not allow themselves to be moved.
Neither the pleasures of the senses, nor frivolous fears,
   Can shake their heart;
This noble memory with which they are imprinted,
Creating their firmness, creates also their happiness.

<summary>This page features a spiritual poem illustrating how divine love strengthens the soul against worldly afflictions and storms. It emphasizes that earthly suffering is superficial and temporary compared to the infinite peace granted by God.</summary>
<keywords>spiritual resilience, divine spouse, mysticism, Otto van Veen, emblems, peace through suffering, divine providence</keywords>

<header>E M B L E M S</header>

### X L I V.

->*True Love knows no measure.*<-

Divine Love must be without measure;
      One never fails
      In its divine excesses:
The more violent it is, the longer its strength endures.
When one loves well, one no longer desires a rule <note>The author suggests that formal religious rules or "measures" are surpassed by the intensity of direct mystical love.</note>
      Simple Charity <term>Charity: from the Latin 'caritas', meaning selfless, divine love rather than just almsgiving.</term>
      Joined to Truth
      Takes flight like an eagle,
      Leaving behind everything that is not God,
One wants nothing of anything that acts as a middle ground.
      Ah, when Love is extreme,
      One dies to everything as well as to oneself
And one finds life in this happy death. <note>A reference to the "mystical death," where the ego is surrendered to find union with the Divine.</note>
      Ah, let us always die in this way!
The more sincere and strong our Charity shall be,
      The swifter its effort will be.
Love, by shattering everything, break the thread of my life
      How happy my fate will be,
When by its attraction, Love, powerful and strong,
      Will have snatched it from me without pity.
      Love, Love, nothing limited anymore,
      Engulf me within your Charity.

<meta>catchword: XLV.</meta>

<summary>This emblem describes the boundless nature of divine love, arguing that true spiritual devotion must be immoderate and lead to a "happy death" of the self. It emphasizes that pure charity transcends earthly rules and limits to find union with God.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, Charity, Mystical Death, Self-surrender, Spiritual Excess, Transcendence</keywords>

--- Page 324 ---

### XLV.

->*The winds cause it to increase.*<-

The more I am overwhelmed by worries and setbacks,
The more I feel the sacred fires grow within my heart:
So many horrible torments, so many diverse pains,
Far from afflicting me, finally fulfill my vows.

May your divine breath, most adorable Spirit,
Which appears outwardly to agitate our heart,
Cause within us a delectable calm!
This agitation increases our ardor. <note>The author uses the metaphor of a fire being fanned by wind; though the wind seems violent, it makes the flame burn brighter.</note>

If it is true that in Love the pain is so charming,
What will those torrents of pleasure be like in the heavens,
With which the powerful and sovereign hand of Love
Must fill our desires through divine excesses!

Love, divine Love, whom I reclaim in secret,
How dear your fires are to me! I adore your rigors.
Ah, if I could one day see your holy flame,
While annihilating me, burn in other hearts! <note>The "annihilation" of the self was a common theme in 17th-century mysticism, referring to the soul losing its ego to be filled with God.</note>

Grow, burn without end, without ever being extinguished:
To increase your torments is to increase your blessings.
The harshness of your fires cannot cause fear;
The more one is consumed, the more peace one finds.

O fire that destroys everything, finally destroy my life,
Unite me, I pray you, to my Sovereign Good! <note>term: Sovereign Good (original: souverain Bien). A philosophical and theological term referring to God as the ultimate source of all happiness and virtue.</note>
But I cannot have this fate worthy of envy,
Unless I am reduced by you to being nothing.

<summary>This page presents a poetic meditation on how spiritual trials and "divine winds" serve to strengthen the soul's love for God rather than diminish it. It emphasizes the mystical paradox where being consumed by divine fire leads to ultimate peace and union with the Creator.</summary>
<keywords>Otto Vaenius, Divine Love, spiritual trials, mysticism, sacred fire, Sovereign Good, annihilation of self, peace through suffering</keywords>

# E M B L E M S

### XLVI.

->*Love disdains all else.*<-

When God reveals Himself to the heart,
One feels only contempt for the greatness of the world;
Honors and pleasures cause us horror,
In leaving everything behind, one tastes a peace so profound,
That one would never believe that privations
        Could constitute the true happiness of a soul:
It is nevertheless in the midst of contradictions <note>In this context, "contradictions" refers to external trials, opposition, or the paradox of finding joy in suffering.</note>,
That the soul feels itself burning with the divine flame.

        Yes, the love of poverty
        Brings, along with wisdom,
        Perfect tranquility,
        And true wealth.
    Happy is he who possesses nothing,
Whose detached heart neither wants nor desires
        Anything but the Sovereign Good! <note>original: "souverain Bien" — a philosophical and theological term for God as the ultimate source of all goodness.</note>
        For he never aspires
        To anything but eternity.
    Everything that is esteemed on earth,
        Is pure vanity:
    Trouble is but a necessary effect
        Of greed.

    The poor in spirit <note>A reference to the Beatitudes in the Gospel of Matthew: "Blessed are the poor in spirit."</note> cannot fear
    The loss of what he does not have.
What can be taken from him? And what harm can reach him?
The care of treasures is not his burden.
        His only care is to please
    His Lord, whom he loves purely:
He can think of nothing except to satisfy Him,
And makes his sole pleasure out of His contentment.

    He who does not leave all, says JESUS, to follow me,
        Is unworthy of me:
        He is very far from living
        Who refuses to die to himself. <note>The concept of "dying to oneself" involves the mystical practice of annihilating one's own ego and desires to be filled with God's will.</note>
Man lives and takes pleasure in all that he possesses;
    He lives in me through privation:
        In all his desires he exceeds;
They will all be fulfilled by possessing me.

<meta>catchword: XLVII. This</meta>

<summary>This emblem explores the spiritual paradox that true wealth and peace are found only through the renunciation of worldly honors and material possessions. It emphasizes that "dying to oneself" and embracing poverty allows the soul to be filled entirely by the presence of God.</summary>

<keywords>poverty, divine love, renunciation, detachment, peace, vanity, Jesus, spiritual wealth, poor in spirit</keywords>

--- Page 330 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of emblems by Otto Vaenius, focusing on the soul's mystical union with the Divine. It follows the previous meditations on spiritual love.</meta>

# FROM O. VAENIUS <note>Otto Vaenius (1556–1629), also known as Octavio van Veen, was a Flemish painter and teacher of Rubens. He published famous emblem books where images and poems illustrate moral or spiritual lessons.</note>

### XLVII.

->To see is not enough.<-

Who can remain calm upon seeing you,
    Dear Spouse of my soul? <note>The "Spouse" refers to Christ, following the tradition of the Song of Solomon where the soul is the bride of God.</note>
In you alone have I placed my hope,
I burn with pleasure from your holy flame.
What happiness to be one day entirely filled by you!
    I love you, I contemplate you:
    My God, how sweet these moments are,
    And how my joy is without equal!

The more I see you, the more I feel myself inflamed,
Your divine gaze, while burning me, calms me:
I love without end, without end I wish to love,
Through my fidelity I shall carry away the palm. <note>The "palm" is a traditional symbol of victory and martyrdom.</note>

What am I saying? Ah, my transport robs me of my judgment,
And I was already forgetting my own weakness!
    Lord, sustain my lowliness,
You alone can make one love constantly.

It is upon you alone also, dear Spouse, that I found
    The hope of keeping my faith to you;
I know well my profound misery,
    And so I expect nothing from myself.

It is true that Love gives me a little audacity,
    I feel a new courage:
    But I count upon your grace,
And your truth shall be my only torch.

<sig>XLVIII. To the</sig>

<summary>This page features a devotional poem reflecting on the soul's intense love for the Divine Spouse, emphasizing that human constancy is only possible through God's grace. It uses the "holy flame" imagery common in 17th-century mystical literature to describe spiritual ecstasy.</summary>
<keywords>Otto Vaenius, Divine Love, holy flame, Spouse, fidelity, joy, spiritual weakness, divine grace</keywords>

# EMBLEMS

### XLVIII.

->*To the heart touched by Love, everything can serve as a path.*<-

When one follows Love, no danger causes fear,
->One makes a passage through everything;<-
When one sees all is lost, when one is most to be pitied,
The soul finds the end of the most frightful paths.

->This Lover <note>The "Lover" here refers to the human soul (the Bride) seeking God.</note> without fear knows how to cross the throng<-
->Of the growling waves of the sea in its wrath,<-
->Without a vessel, without masts: her skill<-
Comes from her surrender to the care of her Spouse <note>The "Spouse" represents the Divine or Christ.</note>.

->These terrible reefs cause her no pain,<-
->She disdains even to look at them:<-
That which creates her rest is that she is very certain
->Of His goodness, and of His power:<-

The less we think of ourselves, the more His providence
->Accompanies us step by step:<-
->Let us increase our confidence,<-
->His care will not fail us.<-

<term>Love (Amour)</term>: In this mystical context, Love refers to the divine attraction between the soul and God.
<term>Providence (providence)</term>: The protective care of God as a spiritual guide.
<term>Confidence (confiance)</term>: A state of total trust and surrender.
<term>Spouse (Epoux)</term>: A common mystical metaphor for the Divine.
<term>Waves/Sea (flots/mer)</term>: Symbols of the world's chaos and life's tribulations.
<term>Rest (repos)</term>: The internal peace found through faith.

<summary>Emblem 48 illustrates the soul's journey through life's dangers, using the metaphor of a woman crossing a stormy sea without a ship to show that total trust in Divine Providence overcomes all fear. It emphasizes that self-forgetfulness leads to greater divine protection.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Providence, spiritual marriage, trust, soul's journey, mysticism, surrender, peace</keywords>

# FROM O. VAENIUS.
<note>Otto van Veen, known as Vaenius (c. 1556–1629), was a Flemish painter and teacher of Rubens; his emblem books combined art with moral philosophy.</note>

## XLIX.

->*Love is a true salt to the Soul.*<-

Salt has been from all time a symbol of Wisdom;
Charity seasons our actions,
Giving to our affections
Both incorruptibility and delicacy.

Wisdom and Love accord well together,
The former leads it straight to the Sovereign Good,
And turns the human heart away
From those deceptive charms that the universe gathers.

Love, like a pure fire, mounts straight to its
sphere,
It finds nothing here below
Where one might turn one's steps,
Everything is poisoned: if it seeks to satisfy itself
It encounters death,
But if it exerts its effort
It surpasses all things,
It stops at nothing, it goes even to its God;
This admirable fire
Rising back to its cause,
Finds in Him, without any defects,
Its purity, its strength, and its rest.

Wisdom is a salt, whose strength is extreme,
Without it everything is insipid and groveling:
He who lacks the salt of Love, if he claims to love,
His speech is bland, and it is nothing but wind.
Deceived by his own reason,
Bitterness appears sweet to him, and sweetness a poison.

Wisdom and Love make the salt of our soul,
They render it of an exquisite taste.
And all goods are acquired by us
If we know how to use the divine flame.

<summary>This emblem explores the metaphor of salt as wisdom and love, arguing that spiritual devotion purifies human actions and directs the soul toward God. It emphasizes that without the "salt" of divine love, human reason is easily deceived and life becomes tasteless.</summary>
<keywords>Wisdom, Love, Charity, Sovereign Good, God, divine flame, Vaenius, spiritual alchemy, salt symbolism</keywords>

# E M B L E M S

## LI.

### L.

->*He drives away all fear.*<-

Perfect Love banishes every kind of fear:
->He inspires sentiments<-
->In his true Lovers<-
Where fear cannot strike a single blow.
It is certain that fear is born of distrust:
->When one is filled with faith<-
->One fears nothing for oneself;<-
Pure Love is followed by faith and confidence.
Love is exalted, it gives true courage,
->And pours out favors<-
->Richly upon great hearts;<-
->Strength is their portion.<-
His heart is generous; his soul, a great soul,
->No timidity at all,<-
->Liberality<-
->Is what he recommends.<-
Love, divine Love, grant me bounty;
->Since an expanded heart<-
->Has at all times made itself<-
->An enemy of all (a) laziness.<-

---

(a) <note>original: "paresse"</note> Perhaps meanness <note>original: "bassesse"; the author suggests a possible correction or alternative reading of the manuscript here.</note>

<summary>This emblem explores the theological and moral concept that perfect love casts out fear, fostering courage, faith, and a generous spirit in the believer. It emphasizes that a "great heart" is defined by its strength and its rejection of spiritual sloth or meanness.</summary>
<keywords>divine love, courage, faith, generosity, fearlessness, moral emblems, virtue</keywords>

# OF O. VAENIUS.

### LI.

->In Him is all happiness.<-

What contentment! How happy is this friend <note>The "friend" or "beloved" refers to the human soul, often personified as a bride in mystical literature.</note>;
To despise everything that is not God!
What joys she tastes in this place!
How delicious is her life!
By leaving everything behind, one unites without intermediary
To this Spouse so dear, with whom the soul is in love.
She has no care now but that of pleasing Him,
Trampling underfoot both the world and the flesh:
To better approach Him,
And to satisfy Him,
She comes to hide herself
In this solitary place.
There, finally separated from all that the world admires,
She shows her fires <note>Her spiritual passion or ardor.</note> to her divine Lover,
Describes to Him her contentment,
Her longing, and her sweet martyrdom;
That she is His, whom she loves uniquely,
That for Him her heart lives, moves, and breathes.
The Spouse, charmed by her vows and her tears,
Embraces her, and leaves her no more,
Fills her with a thousand virtues,
Increases her ardor by showing her His charms.
Here, all memories are rendered superfluous,
From this happy abode all alarms are banished.
Forgetting everything, one leaves oneself behind,
One abandons oneself to this noble ardor:
With God possessing the heart,
One can taste nothing but His extreme Love:
One despises every other honor
Save for that of the supreme Monarch alone;
And the heart finds in Him
Its strength and its support,
When it truly loves.
Love, hope, and faith
Shall alone be my law forever.

<sig>O 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: LII. THE</meta>

<note>Vocabulary: happiness, Spouse, Lover, virtues, Monarch, faith, contentment, support</note>

<summary>This poem describes the soul's retreat into solitude to find union with God, portraying the relationship as a mystical marriage between a Bride (the soul) and a Divine Spouse.</summary>
<keywords>mysticism, divine love, solitude, spiritual marriage, Otto van Veen, virtue, devotion</keywords>

# EMBLEMS
LIII. n

### L I I.

->*Conscience bears witness to it.*<-

What a holy science it is
To listen with care to what God speaks to the heart,
And not to neglect the *synderesis* <term>synderesis: the innate moral spark or "spark of conscience" that directs us toward good</term> and the sorrow
Of our own conscience.

She is at all times a faithful counselor,
Sure, and one who never deceives:
Our soul is cruel to itself
By not listening to either its turmoil or its peace.

When I follow her voice, I find myself tranquil,
My heart is agitated when I follow her not:
Certain deep remorses, a subtle pain,
Make me feel clearly enough when I go astray, alas.

All my happiness depends on hearing and following her;
Woe to him who tramples upon her:
Despite our efforts, she brings things back to life,
And our labors to stifle her are in vain.

When one follows her, one no longer feels a burden,
One lives content in sincerity;
And there our soul finds its freedom,
While serenity lives upon our brow.

God, who placed her within us, desires that she be heard;
She always tells us the truth:
And she would leave no room for doubt,
Were it not for our own infidelity.

<note>The author personifies Conscience as a female guide or counselor who acts as God's internal representative.</note>

<summary>This emblem explores the role of conscience as a divine internal compass, arguing that spiritual peace is only found by listening to this "holy science" rather than suppressing it.</summary>
<keywords>conscience, synderesis, inner peace, remorse, divine guidance, morality, sincerity</keywords>

### LIII.

->*He abhors pride.*<-

To belong to God, profound humility
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Is the surest means:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;God wishes for us to be as nothing,
And yet pride <note>original: "la superbe," referring to haughtiness or arrogant display</note> pleases and reigns in the world.
JESUS CHRIST was the first to choose lowliness,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Contempt was his passion,
Poverty the object of his affection;
This was his doctrine and his high wisdom.
Pride alone displeases Him, and banishes Him from our soul,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Arrogance fills Him with horror;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He delights in a heart
When it is humble and pure; His Charity inflames it.
He leads and teaches it, He warms and enlightens it,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He never abandons it,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Crowning it with a thousand blessings;
In short, the humble and small make themselves loved and please Him.

<vocab>humility, pride, arrogance, Jesus Christ, lowliness, poverty, wisdom, Charity, blessings</vocab>

<summary>This page features a poem emphasizing that humility and the rejection of worldly pride are essential for a relationship with God, citing the example of Jesus Christ's own lowliness. It contrasts the world's love of status with the spiritual rewards granted to the humble heart.</summary>
<keywords>humility, pride, imitation of Christ, spiritual poverty, divine love, Otto Vaenius, emblems</keywords>

# E M B L E M S

### 54.

->*He takes care to instill His laws.*<-

God, through a kindness that has no equal,
Comes to seek me in my error and instructs me;
He opens my eyes, teaching me in a quiet whisper,
Commanding that in secret I love and contemplate Him.

Of His most divine law He shows me the book:
"This is the object," He tells me, "of your faith;
Listen to it, leave everything, and follow me;
Practice these counsels, and you shall be able to follow me:
Renounce all pleasures, embrace virtue,
Let your heart not be cast down by misfortunes;
Die to yourself <note>A reference to the mystical concept of "mystical death" or self-abnegation to live in God.</note> so that you may better live again."

Never weary of admiring and seeing
The excess of my Love, and what my power is;
Look upon my blessings, listen to my words,
Banish far from your heart so many frivolous designs;
Think only of pleasing me, and your generous heart
Will find that it is I alone who can make it happy.

Deprived of all worldly goods, it shall have abundance:
When more misfortunes overwhelm your senses,
And you see your years flow by in harsh labors,
You shall then taste what my mercy can do.

I calm your spirit, I undermine your grief,
I soften your boredom, and I charm your heart;
Against your enemies, I alone am your defense:
Nothing can escape my extreme Love;
Think only of loving me, of seeking my favor;
And then, remain in peace, sure of my providence.

<sig>55.</sig>
<meta>catchword: Who</meta>

<summary>This emblem depicts an intimate dialogue where God instructs the soul to renounce worldly pleasures and find abundance through divine law and providence. It emphasizes the mystical path of self-denial as the gateway to true spiritual peace and protection.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Law, Providence, Self-Abnegation, Spiritual Instruction, Mercy, Virtue, Mysticism</keywords>

# FROM O. VAN VEEN.

### LV.

->*He who does not love, remains in death.*<-

Without Divine Love <note>The author uses "Amour" to represent the personified Divine Love, often depicted as the Infant Jesus or a celestial Cupid in these emblems.</note> our heart cannot live:
->Cold, languishing, and dead,<-
->It cannot, by any effort,<-
->Rise up, hear Him, and follow Him,<-
Unless Divine Love, moved by our miseries,
Comes to withdraw us by His all-powerful arm
->From the weak and languishing state<-
Where we are reduced by our first faults <note>A reference to Original Sin.</note>.
->But His unparalleled charity<-
->Urges Him to seek us out;<-
->He comes to touch us with His arrow,<-
By wounding our heart, He opens our ear.
Come, O Divine Fire, which nothing can extinguish,
->Set ablaze, set ablaze my heart;<-
->You alone are its conqueror,<-
->And You alone can reach it.<-
You alone can wound it with Your arrows;
->It is, it is Yours,<-
->Love, Divine Spouse!<-
->Never close up its wounds <note>The "breaches" or wounds of love are desired by the mystic as they represent an open heart.</note>.<-

<meta>Catchword: LVI. The L</meta>

<summary>This page features a poetic meditation on the necessity of Divine Love to revive the human soul from a state of spiritual death and original sin. It uses the imagery of a "Divine Spouse" wounding the heart with arrows of charity to awaken the believer's senses.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, spiritual awakening, original sin, Otto van Veen, mysticism, charity, Divine Spouse</keywords>

->E M B L E M S<-

### L V I.

#### *Love unites those who are alike.*

Divine Love overwhelms us with favors:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;How lovely are his caresses!
But in order to enjoy these delectable goods,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We must give him our hearts;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And give them in such a way,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;That we reserve nothing for ourselves:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;When his Love transports us
He gives us his heart, and makes our own his.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In a moment he repays our worries and our <term>crosses<note>In this context, "crosses" refers to the trials, burdens, or sufferings a person must endure in life.</note></term>,
He carries us in his bosom, he makes our tears dry up;
He makes us forget so many diverse pains,
Through the outpouring of his holy sweetness.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;O my divine Spouse, whom I love and whom I adore,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Be my only support:
I love nothing but you, and I desire still
To love you more, O my sovereign Good.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Let me be entirely yours, and not my own,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Let me never leave you:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The goal toward which my wishes tend
Is to unite myself to you through an extreme Love.

<summary>This emblem describes the mystical union between the soul and Divine Love, emphasizing the total surrender of the heart to God in exchange for divine comfort and peace.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, spiritual marriage, devotion, mystical union, sovereign good, sacrifice of the heart</keywords>

# OF O. VAENIUS. 113

### LVII.

*Of all the Virtues, it is the foundation and the source.*

Flow, divine waters, through my mouth into my
->heart;<-
I find in you everything that I desire,
For all the virtues for which my heart sighs,
Are given by drinking this sweet liquor.
Faith, Charity, so fertile in every good,
Hope, humility, strength and sweetness,
->Are found within your waves.<-

You quench my thirst, I love nothing else in the world;
The more I drink of you, the more I feel myself burn:
A fire entirely divine, a source entirely fertile,
I taste in you blessings of which I cannot speak:
This excellent beverage,
Teaches us a language,
Known but to very few:
I feel my fire grow
The more I quench my thirst:
It is an admirable mystery <note>original: "mistere"</note>;
This fire has nothing painful
For a heart in love.

When one drinks from this fountain,
The harshest torments cause no pain,
The more one endures, the more one thirsts to suffer:
Divine Love <note>The personification of "Amour divin," often depicted in these emblems as a winged child or Cupid-like figure representing God's love.</note> has so many charms,
That one finds a pleasure in tears;
And one dies of regret at being unable to die.

<header>EMBLEMS</header>

# LVIII.

->*It shall live without ceasing.*<-

Every love that is not pure and divine Love
Cannot last for long: if it captures our soul,
We see it weaken, change, and finally go out;
It is not so with the celestial flame;

It lasts and increases: and immortality
Is the eminent character of this sacred fire;
It burns in time and in eternity,
With its sweet warmth it heats and enlightens.

It never destroys its subject while burning,
It serves as its nourishment, preserves its life;
It is its goal, its end, as it is its object,
And causes a holy pleasure with which our soul is ravished.

This fire, always mounting, rises into the heavens,
Nothing makes it lean toward the earth:
The heart that possesses it—oh precious treasure!—
Makes this sovereign good its only concern:

He sees everything taken from him: liberty, goods, honor,
Yet he makes of this his happiness, he makes it his wealth;
In losing everything, he tastes a certain flattering pleasure,
Which makes him admire the divine Wisdom <note>The original "Sagesse" refers to Sophia or Divine Wisdom, a personification of God's creative and guiding intellect.</note>.

Burn me, divine fire, do not spare my heart,
Break, crush, destroy, you could not do better;
From the harshest torments I will make my happiness,
I count them as nothing; Love, I wish to please you.

<meta>catchword: LIX. It is</meta>

<summary>This emblem describes the eternal and transformative nature of divine love, which unlike earthly passion, sustains the soul even through suffering and the loss of worldly goods.</summary>
<keywords>divine love, immortality, celestial flame, spiritual purification, divine wisdom, eternity</keywords>

<header>OF O. VÆNIUS.</header>

## L I X.

*It is the goal of Love, to make of two but one.*

This is the end of all things,
It is the goal of all our desires:
Admirable metamorphosis! <note>A transformation of the soul's nature through divine grace.</note>
The height of innocent pleasures!
Unity that the Son requested of his Father
For his beloved Disciples! <note>A reference to the Gospel of John 17:21, "that they all may be one."</note>
Chaste bond! Adorable mystery!
Sweet hope of the Predestined!

Who could hope for so great an advantage,
If you had not promised it to us?
It is the sublime and excellent portion
That you give to your friends.

Who could think it, much less claim it?
The All is willing to unite with nothingness;
The sovereign Lord with a little ash,
A drop to its Ocean.

->To lead us to the Heavens,<-
->He willed to descend from them:<-
Abandoning his glory, he makes us glorious.
I lose myself, and cannot understand,
Lord, the excess of your Love.
Permit me to say it to you:
I am a wretch, even unworthy of the light of day,
Yet you share your Empire with me.
You do even more; you give yourself to me,
And your extreme Love,
Makes you change me into yourself;
Your goodness astonishes me and fills me with awe,
You forget what you are,
But I cannot forget who I am:
I revere what you do;
Happy are those who are united to you!

<sig>P 2</sig> <meta>catchword: LX. It is</meta>

<summary>This poem explores the mystical concept of "unitive love," where the soul is transformed and merged with God, emphasizing the humility of the Divine in descending to reach humanity.</summary>
<keywords>mystical union, divine love, metamorphosis, humility, salvation, Otto van Veen, spiritual poetry</keywords>

# E M B L E M S

### LX.

->*It is the fulfillment of the Law.*<-

Who could express the admirable happiness
That a heart led here by Love enjoys!
It has found everlasting rest,
Free from boredom, fear, and worry:
All is calm, all is tranquil:
One desires nothing but God, whom one loves uniquely.
He is the firm support, as well as the sure asylum,
One finds everything in Him: true contentment,
The unchanging peace of which the Gospel speaks,
Which surpasses all feeling <note>original: "sentiment"; likely referring to human understanding or sensory perception</note>,
Which makes the commandment easy,
The path of virtues straight, smooth, and entirely charming.
After one has made a study of the virtues,
One finds in Charity <note>term: Charity (Caritas). In this context, it refers to the highest form of Christian love—the selfless love of God and neighbor.</note>
That admirable fullness
Which places our spirits within the truth:
Its light easily dissipates every cloud
Produced by vain error:
Sacred Love gives here the advantage
Of drinking in long draughts the celestial sweetness.
If one already experiences such a sweet life,
What must eternity be?
With what delights shall it be filled?
A Good, which is never limited!
The soul then, enraptured in its God,
Possesses immortality.

->T H E  E N D.<-

<note>The vocabulary list below highlights the core theological virtues and states of being explored in this emblem.</note>
Law, Love, rest, God, Gospel, Charity, truth, eternity, immortality

<summary>This final emblem celebrates the soul's union with God through Divine Love, which is described as the ultimate fulfillment of religious law and the source of eternal peace. It portrays the transition from the disciplined practice of virtue to the effortless joy of spiritual perfection.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, Charity, spiritual rest, fulfillment of the law, eternity, peace, mystical union</keywords>

### of the preceding POEMS.

<meta>This page serves as a technical guide for the reader, explaining the cross-referencing system between the poetic emblems and their corresponding illustrations found earlier in the volume.</meta>

->The capital numerals **I. II. III. IV. etc.** which<-
->are at the top of each of the following pages,<-
->mark the number of the Emblems; and the<-
->small numeral which is found on the same line<-
->with the capital, marks the page among the<-
->preceding pages, where one will find the figure <note>original: "figure" - referring to the engraved illustration</note><-
->which corresponds to the verses of the Emblem that<-
->one has in view.<-

<note>The author is providing a navigational key for the reader. In 17th-century emblem books, the "Emblem" consisted of three parts: the "motto" (title), the "icon" (the image or figure), and the "epigram" (the explanatory verses). This page explains how to link the text back to the images.</note>

<summary>A brief instructional preface explaining the numbering system used to link the emblem verses to their corresponding illustrations located earlier in the book.</summary>
<keywords>emblems, navigation, cross-reference, book design, poetry, illustrations</keywords>

# P R O L O G U E .

->Here is represented the most charming conversation<-
->Between the Beloved and her Lover;<-
->There, their mutual caresses:<-
->What sweetness, what tenderness!<-
I see on the other side hardships and sorrows,
Dangers overcome, sadness, and tears;
One sees there battles, the abyss, and shipwreck,
The winds, the tempest, and the storm.
But to what will so many diverse torments lead?
To a contentment that surpasses my verse.
The Groom appears jealous <note>In this mystical context, "jealousy" refers to a divine, protective zeal rather than a human flaw.</note> of his most chaste Bride;
She, for her Groom, is jealous even of herself:
She bears his yoke, which seems quite sweet to her
Coming from the hand of the Groom:
And fatal anxiety
Does not disturb her solitude:
Alone-to-alone with God, what innocent pleasures!
What languors, what sighs!
Everything ends at last in the perfect union,
Which comes from the entire defeat
Of the senses, of reason, and of the will;
Everything is reduced to unity.
Divine Charity <note>original: "Charité"; here meaning "Caritas" or divine love.</note>, you performed this great work;
It is from you, it is from you, that the soul has the advantage
Of pleasing her celestial Groom,
And of tasting a good so sweet.

<summary>This prologue introduces a mystical poem or play depicting the soul's journey through suffering and storms toward a perfect, unitive love with God. It emphasizes the surrender of the senses and will to achieve divine union through Charity.</summary>
<keywords>mystical marriage, Bride and Groom, divine union, Charity, spiritual struggle, solitude with God, surrender of will</keywords>

# E M B L E M S

<margin>page 57.</margin>

->I.<-

->*We must love God above all things.*<-

O Supreme grandeur, immense Truth,
Which no one can conceive or understand!
Sublime depth, abyss of beauty,
Grant that our hearts may surrender to your attractions!

You are above the most sublime Love:
The most perfect Love feels its own weakness <note>original: "défaillance" — a sense of fainting or falling short in the presence of the divine</note>,
It sees itself as very small; but it hopes one day
To be able to lose itself in your super-essence <note>A theological term referring to God's being which exists beyond all created categories of existence</note>.

What joy I have, O God, to know you are so great,
That neither faith nor Love can reach you!
I sink and lose myself in a vast nothingness <note>original: "néant" — referring to the "cloud of unknowing" or the stripping away of the self in mystical union</note>;
There I can contemplate, and love you without fear.

<summary>A mystical poem or emblem reflecting on the infinite nature of God, expressing the soul's desire to be consumed by divine beauty and the joy found in God's incomprehensibility.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, mysticism, transcendence, surrender, spiritual beauty, nothingness</keywords>

--- Page 374 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic reflections based on the emblems of Otto van Veen (O. Vanius), specifically focusing on the soul's conversion and gratitude toward the Divine.</meta>

# OF O. VANIUS. <note>Otto van Veen (1556–1629), also known as Vaenius, was a Flemish painter and emblem book author whose work "Amoris Divini Emblemata" (Emblems of Divine Love) is the source for these verses.</note>

## II.

->p. 58.<-

### *We must begin.*

You have rescued me from my wandering,
&nbsp;&nbsp;You have sent me your pure light,
When I found, alas! my entire contentment
&nbsp;&nbsp;In that which could displease you:
When I was plunged into the abyss of evils
&nbsp;&nbsp;On the verge of a sorrowful shipwreck,
Taking me by the hand, you drew me from the waters
When I was about to feel the rage of the rebellious waves.

What do I not owe you for so great a benefit?
I offer you, Lord, both my soul and my life.
Punish, I desire it, my insolent crime, <note>original: "forfait" — a serious transgression or misdeed.</note>
Provided that my soul remains always subject to you.

Ah, never suffer that she <note>The soul is traditionally referred to in the feminine "elle" in French.</note> should be far from you!
She belongs to you, her Savior and her Father:
Let her experience your just wrath instead,
Than to be able to displease you for even one more moment.

<sig>Q 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: III. THE-</meta>

<vocab>Lord, Savior, shipwreck, contentment, crime, wandering, light, wrath, subjected</vocab>

<summary>A poetic prayer of gratitude and repentance where the speaker thanks God for rescuing them from a spiritual "shipwreck" and offers their life in total submission. It is part of a series of reflections on the emblems of Divine Love by Otto van Veen.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, repentance, salvation, Otto van Veen, emblems, spiritual shipwreck, submission to God</keywords>

<margin>p. 59.</margin>

### III.

*Adoption comes from Love.*

Who would believe it, Lord? after so many kindnesses,
Which I acknowledged only with ingratitude,
->You take me, you adopt me,<-
->You dispel my dark anxiety.<-
At the height of the pain of a stinging repentance,
->Caused by my former life,<-
->You adopt me as your child,<-
You calm my heart, and my liberated soul
->Finds that in an instant you break its bonds.<-
Yes, this shrunken heart finds itself almost immense,
->And you have filled it with good things;<-
It tastes the presence of its God in every place.
->Sovereign Good, sweet Paternity,<-
->First fruits of a celestial pledge,<-
->Beginning of truth,<-
I taste you already as the happy portion <note>The "portion" or "inheritance" refers to the biblical concept of God being the believer's prize (Psalm 16:5).</note>
That God promises to those who leave everything for him,
->Who, renouncing every other heritage,<-
->Take Him alone as their sole support.<-

<meta>catchword: IV. The A</meta>

<summary>This emblem reflects on the divine adoption of the soul, expressing wonder that God accepts the repentant sinner as a child and provides a sense of spiritual liberation and peace.</summary>
<keywords>adoption, divine love, repentance, spiritual liberty, fatherhood of God, inheritance</keywords>

# IV.
### p. 60

### *Love is upright.*

Pure and perfect Love is an upright flame,
->Which leans toward no side;<-
->This Love possesses what it desires<-
By wishing, my Lord, for nothing but your will.

This entirely divine Love has but one lovable object,
->God alone is its strength and its weight;<- <note>The "weight" of love is a reference to St. Augustine’s "Amor meus, pondus meum" (My love is my weight), meaning that which draws the soul to its natural place.</note>
Everything that is not God appears detestable to it,
->It is fixed in its first choice.<-

Pure, clear, and disengaged from human nature,
->It tends unceasingly toward this sublime Object,<-
Without bending toward itself, nor toward the creature; <note>The "creature" refers to any created thing or being other than the Creator.</note>
That which is not its God seems too abject to it.

It rises in its heart above itself,
->With a rapid flight it traverses the heavens;<-
->It is with a jealous love that it loves<-
->This noble and glorious object.<-

It could not suffer any leaning, nor any sharing,
Cruel, pitiless, it strips away everything.
Understand, or at least believe this sublime language;
->Experience it: pure Love can do all things.<-

<sig>Q 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: V. L'A-</meta>

<summary>This page presents the fourth emblem of Divine Love, characterizing it as an upright, unwavering flame that seeks only God's will and rejects all earthly attachments. It emphasizes the total devotion and "jealous" exclusivity required for the soul to rise toward the divine.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, Otto Vaenius, spiritual emblems, God's will, mysticism, purity of soul, Augustine's weight of love</keywords>

# EMBLEMS

<margin>p. 61.</margin>

->V.<-

->*Love is eternal.*<-

A hundred times I swore to you an eternal Love,
Divine Spouse, who ravishes my soul:
You told me: it is I who can make it so,
And cause you to burn with an immortal flame.

> I know it, my Lord, I replied in that instant,
> I rely only upon you;
> Make my heart always constant,
> And teach me how one loves you.

Love <note>In this context, "Love" refers to the personification of Divine Love or Christ himself.</note> at that moment came, approached me,
Making an indivisible circle;
This circle is pure Love, and the darkest faith,
Which can admit nothing of the senses <note>The "darkest faith" refers to the mystical concept of the "dark night," where faith operates beyond physical feelings or intellectual understanding.</note>.

> Yet, dear Love, I perceive in your eyes
> An indescribable something <note>original: "un je ne sai quoi"</note> which enchants me;
> A delicious language
> Carries away in an instant the heart of your lover.

> You hold her hand, and by sweet smiles
> You soothe her stinging pains:
> You appease all her worries;
> And her tears, far from being in vain,
> Cause her infinite blessings.

<meta>catchword: VI. L'A-</meta>

<summary>This page features a mystical poem (Emblem V) depicting a dialogue between the soul and the Divine Spouse regarding the nature of eternal, pure love. It uses the symbol of an indivisible circle to represent a faith that transcends sensory experience.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, spiritual marriage, mysticism, eternal flame, pure faith, Divine Spouse</keywords>

# OF O. VAENIUS <note>Otto Vaenius (1556–1629), also known as Octavio van Veen, was a Flemish painter and emblem book author. This text translates his Latin and Dutch verses into French spiritual poetry.</note>

->VI. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; p. 62.<-

->*The Love of God is the Sun of the soul.*<-

O dark ray of this sublime Love,
You pierce with your arrows to the depths of my
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;soul!
O night, more beautiful than the day,
Which consumes my heart with a secret flame!

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Gentle hand, flattering touch,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Which carries me out of myself!
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I can no longer find my heart,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It has passed into that which it loves. <note>This refers to the mystical concept of "ecstasy," where the soul leaves itself to be united with God.</note>

Was it not enough to see your charming eyes,
Without adding arrows of flame,
In order to carry away your lovers,
And penetrate to the depths of their soul?

What! are so many arrows needed to carry away my
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;heart?
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;A single opening was enough.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You have conquered it, sweet Victor,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;No other wound is required.

<meta>catchword: VII. The L.</meta>

<summary>A mystical poem inspired by Otto Vaenius, describing the soul's ecstatic surrender to Divine Love through the metaphors of light, wounds, and fire.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, Otto Vaenius, mysticism, spiritual marriage, ecstasy, religious poetry</keywords>

->E M B L E M S<-

<margin>p. 63.</margin>

# V I I.

## *Love sees itself crowned with a great reward.*

Love is an infinite good,
Which carries its reward within itself:
Happy is the heart to which it is united,
And which lives under its dependence!

Love is God, who gives Himself to my heart
When I love Him without division:
My salary is His glory and His honor,
Love desires nothing more.

Favors and pleasures, for a generous heart <note>In this context, "generous" refers to a noble or high-born soul in the spiritual sense.</note>
Would be converted into torment:
How much more glorious it is to suffer for Love,
And to immolate oneself as a sacrifice!

I love You for Yourself, O my only hope;
This sovereign Love is a reward
For the lover who performs his duty,
And who finds pleasure in suffering,

Who makes his God share in both blessings and evils,
Whose love is invariable
In sweetness and in labors,
Without discerning the bitter from the delectable.

It is this perfect Love which produces in hearts
The Word-God <note>original: "Verbe-Dieu"; a reference to the Logos or the Second Person of the Trinity.</note> as in the womb of Mary:
Love made her the Mother of her Savior.
How infinite is your power, O Love!

<meta>catchword: VIII. L-</meta>

<summary>This emblem explores the concept of Pure Love, where the act of loving God is its own reward, regardless of suffering or pleasure. It concludes by comparing the soul's internal realization of the Divine Word to the Incarnation of Christ through the Virgin Mary.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, spiritual reward, sacrifice, suffering, Virgin Mary, Incarnation, mysticism, disinterested love</keywords>

--- Page 380 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of spiritual emblems based on the work of Otto Vaenius (Otto van Veen), specifically focusing on the soul's education through Divine Love.</meta>

# O. VAENIUS <note>Otto van Veen (1556–1629), a Flemish painter and teacher of Rubens, whose "Amoris Divini Emblemata" inspired these verses.</note>

## VIII. p. 64.

### *Love Instructed.*

Teach me, my adorable Master,
My heart listens, it is fully prepared;
Your lesson must cause me to be reborn:
Ah, shall I soon be separated from this **self**? <note>The "self" or "moi" refers to the ego or the old nature that the mystic seeks to transcend to find union with God.</note>

And night and day I keep an attentive ear
To whatever it pleases you, Lord, to teach me:
Your hand must write within our heart
That which it must not fail to know.

The law of Love has no other reward
Than Love itself; it contains every good.
He who wishes to turn back
Does not have Love as his teacher or his support.

->It is too little that my law be written in your book,<-
->I must engrave it in the midst of your heart.<-

Divine Love, to you alone I surrender myself,
Act as Master and as Creator.

Give me this Love that you deign to teach me:
Experience is above all else.
Alas, what can I do, I who am nothing but ash?
The slightest setback without you drives me to my end.

---

### *On the same subject.*

Happy is he whom the Lord teaches,
Whom he instructs in his will!
When one knows his truth,
Ah, how one disdains all the rest!

If we listened well in the depths of our heart
To the voice of this charming Teacher,
The most ignorant person
Would in a short time be wise,
And would know the secret of loving God purely.
You alone, divine Love, can make me wise.

<meta>catchword: IX. L'A-</meta>

<summary>This page features two poems reflecting on the soul's desire to be taught directly by Divine Love rather than through external books. It emphasizes the mystical "law of the heart" and the necessity of divine grace over human effort.</summary>
<keywords>Otto Vaenius, Divine Love, spiritual education, mysticism, the self, inner voice, divine law, rebirth</keywords>

## V I I.

### *Love sees itself crowned with a great reward.*

> Love is an infinite good,
> Which carries its own reward within:
> Happy is the heart to which it is united,
> And which lives under its dominion!
>
> Love is GOD, who gives Himself to my heart
> When I love Him without reservation:
> My wage is His glory and His honor,
> Love desires nothing more.
>
> Favors and pleasures, for a noble heart <note>The original "cœur généreux" refers to a soul of high spiritual character or "generosity" toward God.</note>
> Would be turned into a torment:
> How much more glorious it is to suffer for Love,
> And to offer oneself as a sacrifice!
>
> I love You for Yourself, O my only hope;
> This sovereign Love is a reward
> For the lover who performs his duty,
> And who finds pleasure in suffering,
> Who makes his God prevail <note>original: "fait patir," here meaning to allow God to act or endure within the soul</note> in both good and ill,
> Whose love is unchanging
> In sweetness and in toil,
> Without distinguishing the bitter from the delectable.
>
> It is this perfect Love which produces in hearts
> The Word-God <note>original: "Le Verbe-Dieu," referring to the Logos or the Second Person of the Trinity</note> as in the womb of Mary:
> Love made her the Mother of her Savior.
> How infinite is your power, O Love!

# FROM O. VÆNIUS.

### VIII. p. 64.

->*Love Instructs.*<-

Teach me, my adorable Master,
My heart listens, it is fully prepared;
Your lesson must cause me to be reborn:
Ah, shall I soon be separated from this "self"? <note>The author refers to the "self" or "ego" as an obstacle to spiritual union.</note>

And night and day I keep an attentive ear
To whatever it pleases you, Lord, to teach me:
Your hand must write within our heart
That which it must not fail to know.

The law of Love has no other reward
Than Love itself; it contains every good.
He who wishes to turn back
Does not have Love as his teacher or his support.

It is too little that my law be written in your book,
I must engrave it in the center of your heart.

Divine Love, to you alone I surrender myself,
Act as Master and as Creator.

Give me this Love that you deign to teach me:
Experience is above all else.
Alas, what can I do, I who am nothing but ash?
The slightest setback without you drives me to my end.

->*On the same subject.*<-

Happy is he whom the Lord teaches,
Whom He instructs in His will!
When one knows His truth,
Ah, how one disdains all the rest!

If we listened closely in the depths of our heart
To the voice of this charming Teacher, <term>Docteur: used here in the Latin sense of 'doctor' or 'teacher' of the faith.</term>
The most ignorant person
Would in a short time be wise,
And would know the secret of loving God purely.
You alone, Divine Love, can make me wise.

<meta>catchword: IX. L'A-</meta>

<summary>This page features two poems reflecting on Divine Love as the ultimate teacher, emphasizing that spiritual wisdom must be engraved on the heart rather than just read in books. It draws on the emblematic tradition of Otto van Veen to illustrate the soul's total surrender to God's instruction.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, spiritual education, Otto van Veen, mysticism, inner life, rebirth, sacred heart</keywords>

<header>EMBLEMS</header>

# IX.

->*Love is a most dear and precious treasure.*<-

<margin>p. 65.</margin>

Love is my treasure, all my good is in him;
He is my happiness, my wealth;
He is my strength and my support,
Without him I am nothing but weakness.

Riches of this world below, how you disgust me!
Vain honor, you tasteless softness <note>The original "mollesse" implies a life of ease or luxury that weakens the spirit.</note>
By which men are enchanted,
True reproaches to wisdom!

O poverty of spirit, you are my treasure;
It is you who give Love itself:
You cost no effort;
My treasure is in that which I love.

Where I have placed my heart, I have placed all my wealth:
If it is my God who possesses it,
He is everything to me: I want nothing more,
I yield to him all that others esteem.

I find in him honor, goods, and holiness,
My happiness, my center, and my glory;
I find in him the truth;
The rest is gone from my memory.

Contempt is my honor, poverty my total wealth,
My pleasure is found in suffering;
Weakness becomes my support,
Love is my perseverance.

<meta>catchword: X. Lo-</meta>

<summary>A spiritual poem defining Divine Love as the only true wealth, contrasting spiritual "poverty of spirit" against the hollow vanities and riches of the material world. The author expresses that finding God allows one to find honor in contempt and strength in weakness.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, poverty of spirit, renunciation of wealth, spiritual treasure, mystical union, suffering as pleasure</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the series of spiritual emblems based on the work of Otto van Veen (O. Vanius), specifically focusing on the purity of divine love and its reflective nature.</meta>

# OF O. VANIUS. <note>Otto van Veen, also known as Vaenius, was a Flemish painter and teacher of Rubens; his "Amorum Emblemata" was adapted here into a spiritual context.</note>

## X. <margin>p. 66.</margin>

### *Love is pure.*

Love, just like a most pure mirror,
Represents the object to our eyes exactly as it is,
Borrowing the form of that which we love:
When one sees nothing there but God, how the heart is hap-
->py!<-

But the wondrous mirror of sacred Love
Is tarnished by the slightest breath;
A single wandering of the loving soul
Hides that Object which once created its pleasure.

Ah, grant that my heart, like a beautiful mirror,
May depict You without end, rare and charming Object!
This must be the only grace
That a true lover can ask of You.

---

### *On the same Emblem.*

This mirror further represents,
That when the heart is set ablaze
By this beautiful fire that consumes it,
Another heart is ignited
By this penetrating flame;
For the reverberation <term>reverberation: the reflection of light or heat; here, the spiritual "echoing" of divine love from one soul to another</term>
Of a heart already in union
Must set fire to the heart of another lover.

<sig>R</sig>
<meta>catchword: XI. In</meta>

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of a mirror to describe the purity of divine love, warning that the soul's reflection of God is easily clouded by worldly distractions. It also explores the "reverberation" of love, where a soul united with God naturally ignites spiritual passion in others.</summary>
<keywords>divine love, mirror metaphor, Otto van Veen, spiritual purity, union with God, emblems</keywords>

<header>EMBLEMS</header>

<margin>p. 67.</margin>

## XI.

### In unity is found perfection.

The end of pure Love <note>The term "Amour pur" refers to a 17th-century mystical concept of loving God for His own sake, without hope of reward or fear of punishment.</note> is intimate union,
To which this Love leads by broken paths:
The cross and contempt, not glory and esteem,
Is the sacred way; all other is superfluous.

GOD ALONE: a single Love reunites all things:
This unique point is the sovereign good.
Love makes us pass into our sole cause,
Where God, our principle, is the mover and support.

Admirable union of God and the loving soul!
In the end, a divine mingling is made.
The soul, possessing nothing, is firm and content,
Love transforming her into her sovereign Good.

She no longer appears, this cherished Lover,
God alone works within her; and in His unity
She is so completely annihilated <note>In mystical theology, "annihilation" refers to the soul's loss of self-will and ego so that it may be filled entirely by the Divine presence.</note>,
That one no longer discerns anything but Love-Truth.

<meta>catchword: XII. The L</meta>

<summary>This emblem describes the mystical "pure love" that leads to the soul's total union with God through suffering and self-surrender. It emphasizes the concept of divine annihilation, where the individual self disappears into the unity of the Creator.</summary>
<keywords>pure love, mystical union, divine unity, annihilation of the soul, sovereign good, spiritual transformation</keywords>

# O. VAENIUS

### XII. p. 68.

#### *Love has its divine battles.*

Against whom do you fight, too-daring lover?
Against this powerful God who governs the heavens?
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Can a weak and wavering blade of grass
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Resist this Victorious One?

I do not dispute to have the victory;
I know that He alone is powerful and strong.
If I fight, it is only for His glory;
It is for Him alone that I make this effort.

If I could carry away this palm <note>The palm branch is a traditional symbol of victory and martyrdom.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It would be to crown Him with it.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If He already possesses my soul,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Could I want it for any reason but to give it to Him?

Divine Love, carry away the victory,
I yield to you without having fought.
Fight, fight; I know how to draw my glory
From your weakness, and not from your virtue. <note>In this mystical context, "virtue" refers to the soul's own strength or moral power; the soul finds its true glory only when it admits its own weakness and relies entirely on God.</note>

<sig>R 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: XIII. L’A.</meta>

<summary>This page features a poem from Otto van Veen's "Emblems of Divine Love," depicting a spiritual struggle where the soul finds victory through total surrender to God. p. 69.

# EMBLEMS

### XIII.

->*Love loves reciprocity.*<-

We see the combatants of a reciprocal Love;
I perceive various wounds:
They place their contentment
Within their deep openings <note>The "openings" refer to the wounds of love, often interpreted in this period as the soul being opened to receive divine grace.</note>.
Their bodies strewn with arrows,
Their faces smiling
To see a thousand and a thousand breaches in themselves,
Is something truly touching.
Their quiver appears full, with their bow drawn
All ready to let fly again;
My spirit is suspended by it
And I admire that which I do not know:
The Beloved <note>original: "L'Amante" - feminine form, likely representing the Soul.</note> is going to die, the Lover <note>original: "l'Amant" - masculine form, representing the Divine.</note> is immortal;
He wounds to heal; if he kills, he restores life:
Divine Love, no, you are not cruel,
And to die by your hand is my only desire.

<sig>XIV.</sig>
<vocab>Love, reciprocal, combatants, wounds, arrows, quiver, bow, Beloved, Lover</vocab>

<summary>This emblem describes a spiritual battle between the Soul and Divine Love, where wounds and "death" are celebrated as the means to eternal life and union. It uses the classical imagery of Cupid’s arrows to illustrate a mystical, reciprocal relationship between the human and the divine.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, mysticism, spiritual combat, reciprocity, martyrdom of love, arrows of desire</keywords>

### XIV.
->Page 70.<-

*Virtue is but the mark of Love.*

O divine Charity <note>original: "Charité divine"; in this context, Charity refers to the highest form of Christian love (Caritas)</note>, all things must yield to you;
You contain within yourself the purest of virtues.
As soon as one possesses you, O Love,
All that is not you appears superfluous to us:
One suffers a thousand diverse torments with pleasure,
One often strives to increase one's own trial:
Outside of you, all languishes in this great universe,
One prefers your divine justice to all pleasures.
One desires nothing for oneself, one desires all for
->my God;<-
The purest virtue is this supreme Love.
->He who does not burn with so beautiful a fire<-
Will never know, O Lord, how one must love you.

# E M B L E M S

p. 71.
### X V.

->*It is the unanimous concurrence of two wills.*<-

**W**hen our will desires all that God desires,
Weak man is surprised to feel what he is capable of;
The more he is weak in himself, the more he finds in God himself—
When submitted to His will—a supreme strength.
Nothing costs him anything more; pain and torments
Become sources of contentment within the Divine will.
That which causes my grief, that which causes my setbacks,
Is finding within myself diverse <note>The author refers to "conflicting" or "divided" desires that pull the soul away from God.</note> wills.
That which causes all evils is division:
Peace and happiness are found in this union.
Ordain my fate, O Supreme Will,
And I shall always be for You, and against myself.
The harshest torments will not astonish me,
If Your Divine will regulates and guides my steps:
And paths strewn with briars and thorns
Will be, for my Love, divine paths and routes.

<meta>catchword: XVI. It</meta>

<summary>This emblem explores the spiritual peace found when a person aligns their own desires with the will of God, suggesting that internal conflict is the source of all suffering.</summary>
<keywords>Divine will, spiritual union, inner peace, strength in weakness, submission, spiritual discipline</keywords>

# XVI.

->*He looks toward the heights.*<-

My heart turns endlessly toward its divine Sun,
It can no longer perceive anything else:
It incessantly follows this peerless Object,
Which moves it and gives it rest.

When the heart is captivated by the Love of its God,
It no longer finds anything else worthy of love:
By a simple gaze at all times and in every place,
It follows without stopping this adorable Sun.

It thinks only of Him, loving Him uniquely;
Nothing distracts its thought
From this rare and charming Object:
The soul is then unburdened of all the rest.

O sovereign happiness to have nothing left but God!
His Love possesses our soul;
And possesses it without any intermediary <note>The original "sans milieu" suggests a direct, mystical union without anything standing between the soul and the Creator.</note>.
Happy is the one who burns with His flame!

---

->*On the same subject.*<-

The Heliotrope <note>The sunflower or marigold, a common symbol in Renaissance art for the soul following the light of Christ.</note> follows its Sun without ceasing;
My heart follows its God just the same:
His pure and peerless Love
Transforms me into the one I love.

No, I could no longer distract my thought
From this God so perfect, so great;
I am unburdened of that which is not Him:
It is He who creates my every movement.

Immense and powerful Being, adorable Light,
Source of Love and of truth,
By illuminating my heart, You close my eyelids
To all that is merely vanity.

<summary>This page features two devotional poems using the metaphor of the heliotrope flower to illustrate the soul's constant orientation toward God as the "Divine Sun." It emphasizes the transformative power of divine love and the rejection of worldly vanities in favor of a singular spiritual focus.</summary>
<keywords>Otto Vaenius, Divine Love, Heliotrope, Divine Sun, Mystical Union, Vanity, Spiritual Transformation</keywords>

### XVII.

->*It increases beyond measure.*<-

When the heart is pure like a beautiful mirror <note>The original French "glace" refers to a polished mirror, symbolizing a soul that reflects the divine light without distortion.</note>,
And when it constantly exposes itself to its God,
It burns and feels its fire grow,
Its Love becomes effective.

To expose oneself before God, to walk in his presence
Through pure and simple prayer <note>original: "oraison" — specifically referring to contemplative or mental prayer rather than recited liturgy.</note>,
To abandon oneself to his motion,
To join love with perseverance;
One will soon feel the whole heart catch fire:
The fire that comes from heaven is a pure flame.
My heart, let us be set ablaze,
Let us give nothing to nature <note>In this context, "nature" refers to worldly or selfish impulses as opposed to the spiritual grace of God.</note>,
We shall know the great art of loving.

# OTHO VAENIUS.

### XVIII. page 74.

->*Preferable to the love of both father and mother.*<-

When we leave behind for God what we hold most dear,
When flesh and blood <note>A biblical idiom referring to family ties and human nature.</note> can no longer move us,
Love becomes everything to us.
Let us leave behind wealth and parents, everything that is not Him;
When we lose every other support,
Our soul finds its rest in Him.
He gives Himself as the prize for the fidelity
Of abandoning everything to love and follow Him:
By losing everything, one possesses the Truth;
By dying to all things, one learns how to truly live.

### X I X.

->*Love is the bond of perfection.*<-
<note>This title references Colossians 3:14: "And over all these virtues put on love, which binds them all together in perfect unity."</note>

Divine knot of charity,
Inviolable Love, center of unity,
How powerful you are in binding my soul!
I no longer feel the fires of my former flame:
> An infinite Object <note>In this context, "Object" refers to God as the focus of the soul's contemplation.</note> who holds me under His laws,
> A Love without flaw, without desire, and without choice,
> A vast and pure light,
> Binds me incessantly to the First Cause.
> My Love has broken my unhappy bonds,
> In order to bind me with His own.

Since that happy time, no longer belonging to myself,
I belong entirely to the one I love.
Ah! never break these fortunate ties;
Love, I am lost if you abandon me.

### XX. p. 76.

->*He is the conqueror of nature.*<-

Withdraw from me, seductive nature,
You can only provide pleasures in appearance <note>original: "en peinture" - literally "in painting," meaning superficial or illusory rather than real.</note>.

Sacred Love alone can create my happiness:
It is he who satisfies both my soul and my heart.
O you, divine Love, carry away the victory,
Banish this enemy, and that shall be your glory.

Withdraw from me, base and deceitful pleasures,
You come to flatter me, wretched seducers.
Love, the Love of God, makes me faithful,
And this Love becomes an eternal Love.
Be silent, feelings; I desire only faith:
Faith, the cross, and love shall unite me to my King <note>In this context, "King" refers to Christ or the Divine.</note>.

p. 77.
## XXI.

->*He keeps us from evil.*<-

Could I still fear the tempest and the storm,
Since you guard me, O my celestial Spouse? <note>The "Celestial Spouse" refers to Christ, a central figure in the "Mystical Marriage" tradition where the soul is viewed as the bride of God.</note>
I no longer dread hell, nor its rage;
I am in safety when I am near to you.

Can any evils fall upon my head?
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Come, break upon me, tempest,
I shall no longer flee you by night or by day:
I am in safety, I belong to Love. <note>In this context, "Love" is personified as God Himself (Divine Love).</note>

I hear the thunder crashing on every side,
And flaming lightning bolts launched against the earth,
Hail, water, and fire mingle in their turn;
I am in safety, I belong to Love.

If He wishes to see me perish, I shall perish without pain;
Everything is welcome from His sovereign hand.
->Love, dispose of my fate,<-
->Whether for life or for death.<-

You shall not see me rebel against your designs:
Love, pure Love, cannot be unfaithful.
Confound and engulf everything on this terrible day;
I am in safety, I belong to Love.

<meta>catchword: XXII. He</meta>

<summary>This emblem poem expresses the soul's absolute trust in Divine Providence, asserting that even amidst violent storms and the threat of death, the believer remains safe through their union with God.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, Celestial Spouse, spiritual safety, providence, mystical marriage, surrender to God</keywords>

# OF O. VAENIUS. <note>original: "D'O. VANIUS." Referring to Otto van Veen, a Flemish painter and teacher of Rubens, known for his emblem books.</note>

## XXII. p. 78.

->He sows and makes the spirit fruitful.<-

To plow a field, one exerts great effort:
One must open and turn the earth with iron;
The more the iron passes, the more one expects a harvest:
The grain is cast there, and then it is gathered in.
This is how Love <note>In this context, "Love" refers to Divine Love or Caritas.</note> acts upon our heart.
The cross and sorrow serve as the plowing;
Penitence extinguishes all infernal passion:
And man could not do anything more.
Sacred Love spreads the divine seed:
One must let it rest,
He will take care to water it,
He will remove the thorns from it.
Divine Love, it is you who plow my heart,
Overturning it according to your wisdom:
Be then its harvester;
To you alone belongs its harvest, its wealth.
It is for the heart to suffer all the overturnings;
For you to gather its most abundant fruits.

<meta>catchword: XXIII. He</meta>

<summary>This page presents Emblem XXII, using the metaphor of agriculture to describe the spiritual refinement of the soul through suffering and divine grace. It portrays God as a farmer who plows the heart with "the cross and sorrow" to prepare it for the divine seed.</summary>

### XXIII.

->*He disdains hearts that are weighed down.*<-

We can never truly belong to God
Except by overcoming the senses, the flesh, and nature:
To call oneself His lover and burn with His fire
Without dying to oneself each day is nothing but a deception.
It is easy to see that devotion <note>The author uses "devotion" here to mean outward religious practice or sentiment.</note>
Is nothing but a pure illusion
When one is unwilling to renounce oneself:
Jesus Christ has told us: to follow Him here below
One must carry his cross and walk in His footsteps,
One must abandon oneself to His supreme will.
It is in no other way that one loves Him.

<note>The signature "V" at the bottom indicates the gathering or section of the printed or bound manuscript.</note>

<meta>catchword: XXIV.</meta>

<summary>This emblem emphasizes the necessity of self-denial and daily spiritual sacrifice as the only authentic way to love God and follow Christ. It warns that religious sentiment without the "carrying of the cross" is merely a deceptive illusion.</summary>

<keywords>self-denial, divine love, imitation of Christ, asceticism, devotion, spiritual discipline</keywords>

<margin>page 80.</margin>
## XXIV.

->*It makes one very generous.*<-

Love makes one generous; and the noble heart
Dares to possess nothing: everything belongs to the beloved;
To relieve one who is suffering
He would wish to give even himself.

If I do any good, I draw from your treasures,
Divine Love, O inexhaustible source,
For both souls and for bodies!
The heart truly in love seems to me incapable
Of claiming any wealth as its own;
His riches consist of possessing nothing.
Love is his treasure, his happiness, his wealth:
He finds in Love his strength and his wisdom.
When deprived of everything and possessing nothing,
He realizes that Love is his only True Good.

# EMBLEMS

### XXV.

### *Envy is the shadow of Love.*

This frightful shadow, alas, which follows us everywhere,
    Is the effect of jealousy.
    Pure Love displeases the envious;
    He, who produces the happiness of life,
    Is unbearable to their eyes.

Love inspires in the heart another kind of jealousy;
    It is that of his honor alone:
    It is exempt from envy,
    And does not torment the heart at all.

It is a sacred zeal for a Lovely Object <note>The "Lovely Object" refers to the Divine or God in the context of Quietist mystical poetry.</note>,
Whom one would wish to make loved in a thousand different places,
    For this pure, holy, adorable God,
    Who rules this great universe.
    One desires no honor, no victory,
    No happiness, no pleasure, no wealth,
    Except to sacrifice it to his glory:
Such a jealous lover reserves nothing for himself.

Pure Love is also jealous of himself,
He cannot suffer a competitor nor a sharing:
And this jealousy ignites his wrath.
He wants the heart entirely as soon as he engages it;
That without looking at oneself, one loves him uniquely:
    To obtain it, he employs everything;
    He deserves even more,
He expects no less from his faithful Lover <note>original: "Amant" - in 17th-century mystical French, this refers to the soul that loves God.</note>.

<meta>catchword: XXVI. Nothing</meta>

<summary>This emblem distinguishes between malicious envy and the "sacred jealousy" of divine love, which demands the soul's total devotion to God without self-interest. It explores the paradox of a God who is both a selfless object of affection and a demanding, jealous lover of the soul.</summary>
<keywords>pure love, divine jealousy, envy, self-sacrifice, mystical union, sacred zeal, devotion</keywords>

# FROM O. VAENIUS.

### XXVI.
<margin>p. 82.</margin>

*Nothing is heavy to one who loves.*

No, no, Love has no burden too heavy,
The soul that would complain of it is unworthy of him:
For a true Lover <note>The French "Amante" is feminine, likely representing the Soul as the Bride of Christ.</note>
Wants nothing in her labors but Love for support.
How sweet is your yoke, how light your burden! <note>A reference to Matthew 11:30: "For my yoke is easy and my burden is light."</note>
They relieve my heart, far from overwhelming it.
The cross is a secret mystery,
Which must not be too widely revealed.
Everyone flees from it, this salutary cross:
Yet it is the choice of my Spouse <note>Referring to Christ as the Bridegroom.</note>.
I wish to carry it without reward,
And sing in all places that this burden is sweet to me.
