### p. 83.

## XXVII.

->*Love alone is the source of all good.*<-

Love makes all things lovely to united hearts;
This union is the source of every good:
No burden ever overwhelms
When Love is its support.
Hardships become favors, and pain a reward
When one’s spiritual taste is refined;
One finds true happiness in humble patience
When one is fully abandoned <note>The term "abandoned" (abandonné) here refers to the mystical practice of "Quietism," meaning a total surrender of the soul's will to God.</note>.

As one entrusts their conduct to the care of Love,
Nothing causes further distress.
If by You, dear Love, I were to be destroyed,
My heart would not sigh for it.
Would a stray sigh render so pure and perfect
A Lover unfaithful?
Justice was never, never cruel:
One sighs only from love and contentment.

<summary>This emblem explores the mystical concept of divine love as the sole source of peace, suggesting that total surrender to God transforms suffering into joy. It reflects the Quietist spirituality of Madame Guyon, emphasizing "abandonment" to the divine will.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, spiritual union, abandonment, Quietism, patience, suffering as reward, Madame Guyon</keywords>

### XXVIII. p. 84.

->*The blows of Love are very sweet.*<-

Strike, strike, my dear Spouse,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But do not fall into anger:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Ah, I fear your wrath far more
Than all the pains your arm can inflict upon me.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Increase and redouble your blows:
I no longer dread your justice, pure Love <note>The author uses "Amour" (Love) as a name for the Divine or Christ</note>.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;How sweet this punishment appears!
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If you have no other torment,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Who will complain of your rigor?
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It shall never be my heart.
Favorable rigors, too-favorable pains,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Are those which come from Love!
Since he gave for me all the blood of his veins,
May I give for him all of mine in my turn!

# EMBLEMS

<margin>page 85.</margin>

### XXIX.

->*Peace and Love go together.*<-

Alas, for a moment of pain and suffering,
Is this then the happiness you intended for me!
How it surpasses my hope!
Is it thus that you chastise? <note>The author uses "chastise" in the sense of divine discipline or refining the soul through trials.</note>
Come, fall upon me, torments, torrents of pain,
You have nothing that can alarm me.
When we fear, how vain our fears are!
You strike only to make yourself loved.
You make us taste your lovely presence,
You fill our soul with peace.
Let us no longer look upon suffering,
Except as charming benefits.
Desirable punishment!
O blows, fortunate blows,
What are the blessings that you give!
My happiness is inexplicable.

<meta>catchword: XXX. The Spi-</meta>

<summary>This emblem explores the paradoxical relationship between divine suffering and spiritual peace, suggesting that God's discipline is an act of love that leads to inexplicable happiness. It reflects a mystical tradition where trials are welcomed as a means of closer union with the divine.</summary>

<keywords>divine love, suffering, peace, spiritual discipline, mysticism, joy in trials</keywords>

# BY O. VÆNIUS. <note>Otto van Veen (1556–1629), a Flemish painter and teacher of Rubens, whose "Amorum Emblemata" was adapted here into a spiritual context.</note>

## XXX. <margin>p. 86.</margin>

### *Hope nourishes a loving soul.*

Hope nourished me
During my most tender youth,
And the Love that guided me
Was full of delicacy:
But as soon as faith, shining in my mind,
Made me perceive a thousand childish traits,
I wished to leave hope behind
And follow pure Love into a dark night. <note>The "dark night" likely refers to the "Dark Night of the Soul," a concept of spiritual purgation described by St. John of the Cross.</note>

Hope shall be your faithful companion,
Love said to me; flee the sweetness of milk; <note>A biblical allusion to moving from the "milk" of basic doctrine to the "solid food" of mature faith (1 Corinthians 3:2).</note>
Come with me to traverse the countryside:
You must, you must change your heart.
I will make you run to the edges of precipices,
You will fear neither pain nor danger for my sake:
I will make you sing in the midst of tortures,
And that is the path where I wish to engage you.

Divine Love, do what you wish to do;
I will follow you everywhere with an immutable faith:
Only permit that I may hope,
Love, and I deliver myself to you.

<meta>catchword: XXXI. L'A-</meta>

<summary>This page features a poem reflecting on the evolution of spiritual love, moving from the "milk" of youthful hope to a mature, sacrificial devotion that persists even through suffering and "dark nights."</summary>

### XXXI.

->*Love hates delays.*<-

I must no longer think of tasting rest;
->Love makes me run without ceasing,<-
->He cannot endure laziness,<-
He finds all his pleasure in hardships and labors.
->Alas, I have truly changed my pace!<-
->I used to rest night and day,<-
->And gave everything to nature <note>In this context, "nature" refers to the fallen human state or physical desires, as opposed to the "spirit."</note>,<-
->Believing I was giving everything to Love.<-

Walking is rest, my dear Master <note>The "Master" refers to Christ or Divine Love personified.</note> told me;
->Your rest shall be in running:<-
One cannot reach the Sovereign **B E I N G**
->Without advancing, and without suffering.<-

Look at this torrent, whose rapid course
Never stops until it has found the sea.
->Leave, leave behind your timid step;<-
Follow me, I will teach you how you must love me.

# BY O. VAENIUS.

### XXXII. <margin>p. 88.</margin>

->*Love sets all things right.*<-

How many detours Love, how many subtle hiding places,
Did I once use when I was far from you:
A thousand turnings, and thousands upon thousands of attachments,
Which I stole away from my faith.
Pure and divine Love, rectify and mend
That which self-love <note>original: "l'amour propre"; in this context, it refers to the theological concept of prideful self-interest as opposed to selfless devotion to God.</note> has spoiled.
I wished to love you in my own fashion,
And I did not love you according to the truth.

Ah, how little righteousness there is far from Love!
When I see our virtues compared to his measure
I perceive nothing but defects:
Alas, one lives only in the natural state,
When one believes oneself to belong entirely to the Most High!

Our works, our virtues, would appear as little
things
If they were measured well by the yardstick of Love:
We shall only discern the cause of this
By the favor of his great light <note>original: "son grand jour"; literally "his great day," referring to the illuminating clarity of divine truth.</note>.

<sig>S 4</sig>
<meta>XXXIII. it</meta>

<summary>This page features a spiritual poem reflecting on how divine love corrects the deceptive "detours" of self-love and human ego. It emphasizes that human virtues are found wanting when measured against the absolute standard of God's pure love.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, self-love, virtue, spiritual rectification, Otto van Veen, emblems, righteousness</keywords>

# EMBLEMS

XXXIV. Everything

<margin>page 89.</margin>

### XXXIII.

->*He prepares the way for God.*<-

I must walk, to go to my God,
By a path strewn with palms and thorns:
The brambles are for me, everything pleases me in this place,
And I love equally such divine routes:
->How all paths are good to arrive at you,<-
->Divine Object <note>In this context, "Object" refers to the focus of the soul's adoration and desire.</note>, who are my delight!<-
->I do not fear the precipices at all:<-
->To perish while seeking you would be a very sweet fate.<-
->Love <note>The author addresses "Amour" (Love) as a personification of the Divine or the Holy Spirit.</note>, carry away the victory;<-
->I want nothing for myself but toil and pain;<-
->I yield all the glory to you,<-
->Alas, how little that is for my heart!<-

<summary>This emblem illustrates the soul's total surrender to God, expressing a willingness to endure suffering and difficult paths to achieve spiritual union. It emphasizes that the journey toward the divine is rewarding regardless of the hardships faced.</summary>

# FROM O. VÆNIUS <note>Otto van Veen (1556–1629), also known as Vaenius, was a Flemish painter and emblem book author. This text is from his "Amoris Divini Emblemata" (Emblems of Divine Love).</note>

## XXXIV. <margin>page 90.</margin>

### *Everything must return to its first source.*

Adorable principle, and my only end,
I receive your blessings in order to return them to you;
And my heart could never aspire
To anything but the supreme happiness of belonging to its Sovereign.
All good things come from you, and to you they must re-
turn:
If you lavish your favors
Upon weak and cowardly hearts,
Do not allow those favors to dwell there <note>The author suggests that divine gifts should pass through the heart back to God, rather than being hoarded by the ego.</note>.

As for me, my dear Spouse <note>A common mystical reference to the Divine or Christ as the bridegroom of the soul.</note>, I find all my
pleasure
In returning everything to my sole principle:
When the heart is empty of desire
It immediately participates in its sovereign Good:
For by retaining nothing for itself,
It sinks and loses itself in that immense sea;
When it abandons the SELF <note>original: "le MOI"</note>
It is within sacred Love that it makes its residence.

<summary>Emblem XXXIV explores the mystical concept of "reflux," where the soul finds peace by returning all divine blessings to God and abandoning the ego to dwell in sacred love.</summary>
<keywords>Otto van Veen, Divine Love, spiritual source, self-abandonment, mystical union, Sovereign Good</keywords>

# EMBLEMS

<margin>p. 91.</margin>
### XXXV.

->*He is firm & constant.*<-

You have bound me, Love <note>The author uses "Amour" (Love) here as a personified force, likely representing either Cupid or Divine Love depending on the reader's perspective</note>, to this stake,
The flames surround me on every side:
Is there any new torment,
To which my soul would not surrender?

Increase and redouble your fires;
I do not feel their violence at all:
When the heart is truly in love,
The beautiful fire within destroys their vehemence <note>The internal "fire" of passion or devotion is depicted as being so intense that it renders external physical pain or fire unnoticeable</note>.

Love, ah, leave me be, so that I might suffer,
For as long as you sustain my soul
It can neither languish nor die,
And finds delight within the flame.

These horrible executioners <term>bourreaux: executioners or torturers</term> are then your instruments,
And could I still be afraid of them?
Redouble my Love, and let their torment grow,
For their fire is ready to be extinguished by yours;
And I see it as a mere amusement,
Since it cannot yet reach me.

<meta>catchword: XXXVI. THE-</meta>

<summary>This emblem depicts a figure bound to a stake, illustrating the paradox that the internal fire of love or devotion is so powerful it overcomes the pain of external persecution or physical fire. It emphasizes the theme of constancy and the transformative power of passion.</summary>
<keywords>Love, constancy, martyrdom, fire, soul, endurance, spiritual passion</keywords>

<margin>Page 92.</margin>
# XXXVI.

->*Love edifies and builds.*<-

Destroy, dear Love <note>In this context, "Love" refers to Divine Love or the personification of Christ</note>, my old house;
Be the foundation of a new edifice:
    May it be a place of prayer,
Where one offers the eternal sacrifice of the heart.

You do not raise it upon shifting sand;
    But upon the living rock:
        When the flood arrives,
It can never shake it for an instant.

What one does without Love is building upon the sand,
    Where the slightest overflow carries away
        This superficial, this flimsy building.

Our works, our virtues without Love are made of
    straw,
    Which have no value in faith:
Happy are those with whom pure Love works,
Their works, their virtues are worthy of the Lord.

<sig>XXXVII. He</sig>

### XXXVII.

->*He spreads a charming fragrance.*<-

Draw me after you, my divine Spouse,
Said the Bride of the <term>Canticles</term><note>The Song of Songs or Song of Solomon, a book of the Bible often interpreted as an allegory of the soul's love for God.</note>,
The scent of your perfumes, so ravishing, so sweet,
Will carry away the hearts of these modest virgins,
Whose robes cast a radiance of such beautiful whiteness
As they follow you, O divine Lamb.

My soul thirsts for a more exquisite perfume;
Contempt and sorrows are the fruits of this land:
Reserve your charming sweetnesses for heaven,
For here I need only hardships and rigors.

You once blazed the trail of suffering for me,
And you taught me the nature of your patience:
The cross and adversity, for a generous heart,
Make it follow you, divine Lamb, in every place.

When I see my JESUS covered in scars,
Could I amuse myself by tasting delights?
There is no joy for me but to walk in his footsteps
And to suffer like him until my death.

<summary>This emblem uses the imagery of the Song of Solomon to contrast the "sweet perfume" of Christ's divinity with the "bitter" necessity of suffering and the cross in the earthly life of a believer.</summary>
<keywords>Song of Solomon, Bride of Christ, Divine Lamb, suffering, imitation of Christ, spiritual devotion</keywords>

## XXXVIII. <insert>P. 94.</insert>

->*With Love, one is in safety.*<-

I see on all sides a great number of enemies,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Who press upon and surround me:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;They believe they shall make me submit,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;While death and hell dog my heels.
Despite so many dangers, I apprehend nothing;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Let them strike me, let them imprison me:
What is done against me would seem a blessing
If divine Love served as my support.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It is to him that I abandon myself;
In his arms I apprehend nothing.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;There I taste a peace so profound,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;That I would dare to defy the whole world.
I rest upon his breast, and my tranquility
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Comes only from charity <note>In this context, "charity" refers to *Caritas*, or selfless divine love.</note>.
Who can separate me from this lovable Object?
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Neither death nor captivity
Can do anything against the truth;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;She is, against everything, unshakable.

### XXXIX.

->*He quenches the thirst of the heart.*<-

**Y**ou quench my thirst, O my divine Spouse! <note>The "Spouse" refers to Christ, following the tradition of the Song of Solomon where the soul is the bride of the Divine.</note>
How full of bitterness are the waters here below!
In loving you, one tastes a charming and sweet fire
Which consumes us without burning us.
One finds in your breast a peaceful spring,
Entirely full of delight,
Which makes one insensitive to worldly pleasures,
And places us within the truth.
It is you who give us the excellent fountain
That you promised to the Samaritan woman: <note>A reference to the Gospel of John 4:14, where Jesus offers "living water."</note>
It produces within us a gracious river,
Which shall spring up even unto the heavens.
This river is Pure Love, <term>Pure Love: a theological concept (Amour Pur) referring to loving God for His own sake, without hope of reward or fear of punishment</term> which returns to its source;
It banishes self-interested love from our hearts.
He who never interrupts its course,
Will find himself more than rewarded.

<sig>XL. Who</sig>

<margin>page 96.</margin>
## XL.

->*He who wishes to love is no longer free in his own way.*<-

Whoever complains of your burden,
O Love, and of your yoke, has never truly carried it.
Ah, if one fears being weighed down by such sweet slavery,
He is instead a captive to his own greed <note>original: "cupidité" - here referring to worldly desires or lust as opposed to selfless love</note>.
By captivating the heart, you set it more at ease;
You grant it true liberty.

Your yoke appears heavy to the weak and tender soul;
But how light it seems to the heart truly in love,
Which, far from wishing to defend itself against it,
Believes itself a hundred times more glorious for bearing it!

Ah, captivate my heart, sole Author of my flame!
I surrender to you, as to my conqueror,
The rights I once held over my soul;
Be its peaceful possessor.

<note>The "Author of my flame" refers to the beloved or the divine source of inspiration, a common trope in 17th-century emblem books where earthly love (Eros) is often transformed into divine love (Anima).</note>

<summary>This page features Emblem 40 from Otto Vaenius, exploring the philosophical idea that surrendering to love is not a loss of freedom, but the attainment of true liberty. It contrasts the "sweet slavery" of love with the restrictive captivity of worldly greed.</summary>
<keywords>Otto Vaenius, emblems, divine love, liberty, slavery, yoke, desire, soul</keywords>

## X L I.

->*The unique Love shines among the virtues.*<-

Love encompasses the virtues:
Without it, no virtue could ever be pure.
Often our efforts are superfluous;
Believing we follow Love, we follow nature <note>The author distinguishes between "nature" (human instinct or ego) and divine Love (grace).</note>.

There is nothing outside of you, beneficent Charity <note>original: "Charité"; here referring to the theological concept of selfless, divine love</note>,
For your faithful and tender Lover:
I find in you, dear Love, every good thing.
It is you who produces them, it is you who sustains them:

With you, virtue is found without error;
A sincerity that never wears a disguise,
An ingenuity <note>In this context, "ingénuité" refers to a childlike simplicity or lack of guile</note> that never fails;
Everywhere, an equal frankness:
This is the virtue of a heart that leaves itself to your care.

<meta>catchword: XLII. L'A-</meta>

<summary>This emblem illustrates that divine Love (Charity) is the foundation of all genuine virtue, warning that human effort alone is often just "nature" rather than true spirituality. It emphasizes that qualities like sincerity and frankness flow naturally from a heart surrendered to this Love.</summary>

<keywords>Charity, divine love, virtue, sincerity, spiritual simplicity, heart's devotion</keywords>

# O. VAN VEEN

### XLII.
<margin>p. 98.</margin>
->*Love overcomes all.*<-

Come and carry away my heart, O most adorable Love;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;For you, nothing is impenetrable:
The most hardened heart would resist in vain.
You can do whatever you wish, sole Author of my flame:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As soon as you form the design
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;To penetrate the depths of the soul,
One is subjected by your charms so sweet:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;One is wounded by even the slightest blows.

Ah, as soon as a heart of steel receives your arrows;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It immediately changes its nature,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Relinquishing its overly harsh quality
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;When it experiences your attractions,
It feels nothing within itself but perfect desires.

Make, O divine Archer, so many breaches in my heart,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;That by exhausting all your arrows
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;I might likewise in my turn
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Wound you with my chaste love. <note>The "divine Archer" refers to Cupid in a secular context, but in this spiritualized emblem tradition, he represents Divine Love or the Christ-child (Anteros) aiming at the soul.</note>

### XLIII.

->*Shaken, it becomes firmer.*<-

This oak tree that I see beaten by the storm,
Does nothing but grow stronger: its proud head
Appears to brave the impetuous winds,
Stiffening itself within its roots
When these injurious weather-times
Seem to threaten it with a prompt ruin.

It is the same with my heart;
When everyone makes war against it,
And it hears the thunder rumble,
It firms itself against fear.
Watching without turning pale where the storm will fall,
It sustains everything with courage;
It is not at all dejected, nor is it over-bold,
Proud of the help of the heavens. <note>The author suggests a balance of "stoic" endurance and religious faith, where the heart is neither crushed by fear nor arrogant in its own strength.</note>

<sig>XLIV. The</sig>

<summary>This emblem uses the metaphor of an oak tree strengthening its roots during a storm to illustrate how the human heart finds resilience and divine support through adversity. It emphasizes the virtue of constancy and courage in the face of life's "tempests."</summary>
<keywords>emblem, oak tree, resilience, courage, storm, divine providence, constancy</keywords>

# O. VAN VEEN

### XLIV. <span style="float:right">p. 100.</span>

->*True Love knows no measure.*<-

The rule of Love is to love without measure:
Let us break, divine Spouse <note>A common mystical reference to Christ as the Bridegroom of the soul</note>, the rule and the bushel-measure;
->Let us leave time to chance;<-
->Love gives a new pleasure.<-
Let us say and say again, nothing appears so beautiful,
*The rule of Love is to love without measure.*
->Ah, let us never count the hours:<-
The season of Love ought to be eternal.
->Let us speak no more of anything but spring;<-
->Divine Love is the new season,<-
->Of the faithful heart and of constant loves.<-
->Divine Author of nature,<-
You who know so well how to move our heart;
If I can love you with a Love without measure,
I shall soon attain sovereign happiness.

## XLV.

->*The winds cause it to increase.*<-

The more I am agitated, the more I am fought against,
Love increases my virtue <note>In this context, "virtue" refers to both moral strength and the inherent power or "potency" of the flame.</note>:
By the mutinous winds I feel my flame grow,
They make my soul more firm.

Blow from all sides, O impetuous winds;
The more you blow, the more I feel my fires grow.
The torments of Love have nothing but what is agreeable:
Their agitation renders my fire delectable.

->Pour down upon me, torrents of evils,<-
->My fire increases through labors,<-
->I no longer fear your bitterness:<-
->Agitated, I taste a happiness<-
->That my pen cannot depict,<-
->For it is greater than my heart.<-

### XLVI.

->*Love disdains all else.*<-

For you I have despised honor,
And all the riches of fortune.
It is still too little for my heart:
Everything that is not you afflicts and troubles me.
You are not satisfied with what I have left behind,
Unless I also leave myself:
Your Love is full of justice,
It wants everything for the Supreme Good <note>The "Supreme Good" (Summum Bonum) refers to God as the ultimate object of desire and fulfillment.</note>.

Without seeking in Him anything but His own interest,
Without wanting any reward from Love,
Let us always do what pleases Him;
What a noble science <note>original: "auguste science"; here "science" refers to a spiritual discipline or sacred knowledge</note> this is!
Let us not amuse ourselves by seeking sweetness;
Let us desire nothing from God but His unique honor.

<summary>This page features a spiritual poem emphasizing that true Divine Love requires the total renunciation of worldly honors, wealth, and even one's own self-interest. It advocates for a "noble science" of loving God purely for His own sake without seeking personal reward or emotional comfort.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, renunciation, Supreme Good, Otto Vaenius, spiritual discipline, selflessness</keywords>

# E M B L E M S

### XLVII.

<margin>p. 103.</margin>

->It is not enough merely to see.<-

Who could conceive of the sweet contentment
One receives in seeing you, O supreme Monarch! <note>original: "Monarque suprême" — a reference to God as the sovereign ruler of the soul.</note>
Yet possessing you within oneself surpasses
All that can be seen when one loves you.

Ah, close yourselves, my eyes; cease to open;
I desire a Good that surpasses sight:
I contemplate, it is true; but Love wishes to enjoy
The pure and naked truth.

I would wish to plunge myself into his vast breast,
And in him lose myself without ceasing.
How beautiful my lot would be, how happy my destiny,
If, lost within your Wisdom,

I no longer saw myself, and knew nothing
But the totality of this unique Good!

No, thought—too limited—you are not suited
To this adorable Object:
You are too coarse, too imperfect, too low:
Love, pure Love, is alone sufficient. <note>The author argues that intellectual reasoning is "too coarse" to reach God, and only "pure love" (disinterested love of the soul) can achieve union.</note>

<insert>XLVIII.</insert>
<meta>catchword: To</meta>

<summary>This emblem explores the mystical concept that intellectual contemplation and sight are insufficient for divine union, urging the soul to move beyond thought into "pure love." It emphasizes the desire to be lost in the "vast breast" of the Divine rather than merely observing from a distance.</summary>
<keywords>mysticism, divine union, pure love, contemplation, wisdom, spiritual sight, interiority</keywords>

# FROM O. VÆNIUS. 167 XLVIII. p. 104

*To a heart touched by Love, everything can serve as a path.*

Pure and divine Love, you leave your Lover
->At the mercy of the waves: how happy I see her!<-
Buffeted in a hundred ways in the midst of this water,
->Her quiver serves her as a vessel,<-
Her bow as a rudder. There, without fearing the storm,
->She tastes a new pleasure.<-
->From where, then, does this great courage come?<-

It is from Love; it is he who causes these tortures:
She does not even perceive the precipices
->Which surround her on all sides:<-
And without opening her eyes to her approaching shipwreck,
->O, what a happy advantage!<-
->She despises the hazards.<-

It is thus that Love exposes us to danger
->To test our courage,<-
To see if hell, the world, and its rage
Could indeed make us change.

A heart truly in love sees nothing but Love;
->In the most extreme peril<-
->It would not dare upon itself<-
->To sigh or look back.<- <note>The "return upon oneself" implies self-pity or self-preservation, which the devoted soul rejects.</note>

->A N O T H E R<- <note>This second poem offers a more personal, interior monologue of the soul facing the same trial.</note>

I must then cross this stormy sea:
Must I abandon myself to the mercy of the waves?
->Ah, how little courage I have!<-
->I, who loved nothing but rest.<-

I must then cross abyss and precipice,
To be thus the plaything, Love, of your justice?
Are these the great blessings that you promised me?
Do you wish to see me perish? I am almost at my end:

->Love, you laugh at my shipwreck:<-
I feel the waves rising, I hear the storm grumbling,
The sea, in opening up, lets me see nothing
But a profound abyss where I am ready to fall.

Do you treat your so dear lover thus?
Let us perish, I consent to it; I wish to satisfy you;
And without listening further to my insulting tears,
Love, I am going to perish, and perish before your eyes.

<summary>This page features two poems accompanying an emblem by Otto van Veen, depicting the soul navigating a stormy sea using the tools of Love (bow and quiver) as a boat. It explores the themes of total surrender to divine will and the transformation of suffering into spiritual joy.</summary>

<keywords>Divine Love, Otto van Veen, spiritual shipwreck, courage, martyrdom of the heart, sea allegory, devotion</keywords>

# E M B L E M S

<margin>p. 105.</margin>

### XLIX.

->*Love is a true salt to the soul.*<-

Love is the salt of our soul;
Without it, there is nothing but tastelessness;
It is love that preserves the heart,
And which nourishes and inflames it.

The salt of pure Love preserves from within;
It is the spirit of Wisdom:
Of that wisdom which makes us children,
But children of the promise. <note>A reference to the biblical concept of "children of the promise" (Galatians 4:28), implying those born of the spirit rather than just the flesh.</note>

She <note>Wisdom, personified as feminine</note> opposes within us that false prudence
Which is so contrary to the spirit of faith.
When Love holds us under its law,
One loves the indistinct, and flees from the evident. <note>The author suggests that true faith embraces the divine mystery ("the indistinct") rather than relying on human logic or sensory proof ("the evident").</note>

Wisdom consists in giving everything to God,
Without reserving anything for oneself:
This is what is owed to that Supreme Being,
Without which, Love has no place.

<sig>L. ii</sig> <note>Printer's signature mark for bookbinding.</note>

<vocab>Love, Wisdom, God, Supreme Being, faith, promise, salt of the soul, prudence, evidence</vocab>

<summary>This emblem uses the metaphor of salt to describe Divine Love as a preservative and seasoning for the soul, contrasting spiritual "childlike" wisdom with worldly prudence. It emphasizes total self-surrender to God as the essential condition for Love to dwell within a person.</summary>

<margin>page 106.</margin>
### L.

## *He drives away all fear.*

Perfect Love must banish all fear:
It hates all timidity;
And divine Charity <note>The author uses "Charity" in the theological sense of *Caritas*, or selfless, godly love.</note>
Cannot suffer any constraint.

One does everything freely, with great courage,
Carried upon the wings of Love;
One finds a very great advantage
In serving God without fear and without looking back.

What is there to fear, O Lord of my life?
As soon as one abandons oneself to you,
Our soul finds itself set free,
One does not even apprehend your wrath.

Love teaches us how to descend <note>Likely referring to the "descending" path of humility or the soul's submission to the divine will.</note>:
When its fire has reduced us to ashes,
Upon whom, Great God, upon whom could your blows fall?

<sig>T 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: LI. In</meta>

<keywords>Divine Love, Charity, Fearlessness, Spiritual Freedom, Otto van Veen, Emblems, Devotion</keywords>

# E M B L E M S

<margin>p. 107.</margin>

### LI.

->*In him is all happiness.*<-

After so many torments I taste happiness,
->Great God, to be in your presence:<-
->The world is nothing but a deceiver;<- <note>original: "suborneur" – one who misleads or corrupts through false promises.</note>
I fear nothing, for you are my defense.

->When the soul is above the senses,<-
Neither the world nor hell can harm it anymore:
It tastes innocent pleasures with God,
->Which my pen can hardly describe.<-

In these secluded places the soul possesses God,
->Or rather, her God possesses her:<-
It is in this holy wilderness, in this lovely place,
->That she finds the sure remedy for her ills.<- <note>The "wilderness" or "desert" refers to the mystical tradition of seeking God in solitude and silence, away from worldly distractions.</note>

Divine Love, when one lives with you,
->Could one suffer anything?<-
->It is in the bosom of my Spouse<- <note>original: "Epoux" – a common mystical metaphor where the soul is the bride and Christ/God is the Bridegroom.</note>
That I find peace, and that my heart rests.

<sig>LII. The</sig>

<summary>This emblem describes the soul's transition from worldly suffering to divine peace through mystical union with God. It emphasizes that true happiness and safety are found only in the "holy wilderness" of spiritual solitude and the embrace of Divine Love.</summary>

# FROM O. VAENIUS.

## LII. <margin>page 108.</margin>

->*Conscience is the witness.*<-

I see Divine Love presenting me with the cross;
Profane love <note>original: "l'amour profane"; referring to worldly or carnal affection</note> offers delights:
I do not waver over such a worthy choice,
I prefer the most terrible torments over pleasures.
I feel a certain "I know not what" <note>original: "je ne sai quoi"; an indefinable feeling or inner impulse</note>
Carrying me almost despite myself
To prefer what is useful over what is delectable:
My sentiments turn toward what is just.
Without looking to my own interests,
I submit myself, Lord, to all your holy decrees,
I am quite willing to live in suffering for your Name,
To prove my Love to you through my obedience.
We have within us a sovereign Mover <note>A philosophical term for God as the primary cause of action</note>,
Who does not allow us to be taken by surprise;
And this enlightened Director
Never permits us to be mistaken.

<sig>II</sig>
<meta>catchword: LIII.</meta>

<summary>This page features a poetic meditation on the internal conflict between divine devotion and worldly pleasure, asserting that the conscience acts as a God-given guide toward righteousness. It emphasizes the soul's willingness to endure suffering for the sake of divine obedience.</summary>
<keywords>conscience, Divine Love, Profane Love, moral choice, obedience, sovereign Mover, Otto Vaenius, spiritual direction</keywords>

# E M B L E M S

### LIII.

->*He abhors pride.*<-

<margin>p. 109.</margin>

Nothing is more hateful to sovereign Love <note>The author uses "Amour" (Love) as a personification of God or the Divine.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Than the pride of life; <note>original: "la superbe de la vie," a reference to the biblical concept of worldly arrogance.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It increases every day
And makes the soul more enslaved at every moment.
Feeding on everything, even good deeds
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Provide it with a very common meal:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;One sees within acts of devotion
Only pride, and not sincere piety.
Pride grows with us, and follows us to the grave,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;It even increases with age:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Always some new subject
Gives it the advantage over us.
Alas, divine Love, stop its course;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;You alone have the power to do so;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If not, it will follow me always,
It is contrary to my desires.
I see humility full of sweet charms!
I love it, I want it, and I do not find it.

<summary>This emblem reflects on the insidious nature of pride, which can corrupt even religious devotion and good deeds, and concludes with a plea for divine assistance to achieve true humility.</summary>

# BY O. VAENIUS. <note>Otto Vaenius (1556–1629), also known as Octavio van Veen, was a Flemish painter and teacher of Rubens; he published the "Emblems of Divine Love" upon which these verses are based.</note>

### LIV.

-> *He takes care to instill his laws.* <-

You are, dear Spouse, in the depths of my heart;
-> It is there that your law has been engraved: <-
You have delivered me from the seductive spirit,
My soul is entirely yours, you have carried it away.

-> Each day I receive new lessons <-
-> From your divine Wisdom: <-
-> You place me in a hundred different ways <-
-> In order to test my flexibility: <-
-> Sovereign Spouse of my heart, <-
Be always my master and my teacher. <note>The French "docteur" here refers to a teacher or doctor of the faith, one who imparts authoritative knowledge.</note>

-> You teach the truth, <-
-> It is you alone who can do so: <-
-> The rest is but vanity, <-
-> And men must remain silent. <-
I find nothing among them but vain illusion,
Their discourse is filled only with confusion.

---

-> *On the same subject.* <-

I aspire only to the blessing of being instructed by you;
Speak, speak, Lord, my soul listens to you:
What you teach is perfect, it is sweet,
-> And leaves the soul with no doubt. <-

You write your laws in the depths of my heart;
It is this law of Love which gives me life.
-> Love is Master, he is Teacher: <-
The soul observing his law is set free from fear.

Then one is no longer subject to the various changes
-> That the rest of men experience; <-
-> They become perfect lovers, <-
-> Love, within yourself you consume them. <- <note>In this mystical context, "consume" refers to being perfected or completed in divine union.</note>

<sig>LV. Who</sig>

<summary>This page features two poems reflecting on the soul's internal education by God, emphasizing that true wisdom comes from the "Law of Love" engraved on the heart rather than human speech. It draws on the emblem tradition of Otto Vaenius to depict the relationship between the Divine Spouse and the soul as one of master and student.</summary>
<keywords>Otto Vaenius, Divine Love, spiritual instruction, Law of Love, mystical union, internal word, vanity of man</keywords>

### LV.

->*He who does not love remains in death.*<-
<note>This is a reference to 1 John 3:14: "He that loveth not his brother abideth in death."</note>

Sacred Love, you give me life;
Without you I remain in death,
And would not know how to make an effort,
So much is my soul weighed down.

It is you, divine Love, who makes one live and die;
One must die to everything to possess life:
Life is, by death, set free from death;
It is Love that heals the pains it causes to be suffered.
<note>The author uses a common mystical paradox: "dying to the self" or the world in order to achieve eternal life.</note>

O pure Love, how tranquil is your flame
When one gives oneself entirely to you!
When you become the master of our soul,
One follows nothing but the law of love.

<meta>catchword: LVI. The L.</meta>

<summary>Emblem 55 explores the mystical necessity of divine love, arguing that the soul remains spiritually dead and burdened until it surrenders to a "death of the self" that brings true life.</summary>
<keywords>divine love, spiritual death, rebirth, soul's surrender, mystical paradox, sacred love</keywords>

# FROM O. VÆNIUS.

### L V I. page 112.

### *Love reunites like with like.*

Sacred Love makes lovers equal,
And unites them with an eternal chain:
When I see their holy embraces,
I well understand their mutual love.
What, do you humble yourself, my sovereign Lord,
Even to the point of making your poor servant your equal!
This goodness ravishes my heart:
How strong it is, how touching!
You loved me first
With a Love that is pure and freely given;
Grant that my return to you, dear Spouse, be complete,
And that to be yours, I may leave myself behind.
I love you for yourself, O sovereign Author
Of my chaste and modest flame;
Without occupying myself with my own happiness,
I surrender my soul to you.

<summary>This page features a devotional poem based on the emblems of Otto van Veen, depicting the mystical marriage where Divine Love equalizes the soul with God. It emphasizes selfless devotion and the soul's desire to abandon its own interests to be fully united with the "Spouse."</summary>

<keywords>Divine Love, mystical union, Otto van Veen, spiritual marriage, humility, self-surrender, sacred desire</keywords>

## LVII.

### *Of all the virtues, this is the foundation and the source.*

Love is the support of all the virtues,
It contains them within itself, then communicates them to us:
->He who desires nothing else<-
Receives them richly from Love's magnificent hand.
->When I am in Love, I possess them at their source,<-
->All my worldly desires are deadened:<-
When I lack everything, Love is my resource;
->Oh, how happy are those truly annihilated!<- <note>The "annihilated" refers to the mystical concept of "Quietism," where the soul's own will is completely stilled or "annihilated" to make room for God's will.</note>
->I plunge myself into Love, not content merely to drink;<-
->From this bath one emerges pure and clean:<-
I can wish for nothing, pure Love, but your glory,
->Your honor alone satisfies me.<-
->Let whoever wishes seek their own advantage from you;<-
->That thought seems too base to me:<-
->I desire no other portion<-
Than to sacrifice myself endlessly to your divine charms. <note>original: "apas" (appas), meaning attractions or charms.</note>

<vocab>Love, virtues, desires, annihilated, honor, portion, charms</vocab>

<summary>Emblem 57 explores the mystical idea that Divine Love is the singular source of all virtue, leading the soul to a state of "annihilation" where personal desire is replaced by a focus on God's glory.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Love, Virtue, Quietism, Self-sacrifice, Spiritual Purity, Mysticism</keywords>

### LVIII.
<margin>page 114</margin>

->*He shall live without ceasing.*<-

Return, divine Love, this immortal flame,
  You who kindle it within my heart:
  You are its sole Author;
  May our love be eternal!

Could I for a single instant be separated from you,
  Divine possessor of my soul?
  Ah, increase my modest flame:
How sweet such a burning will be to my heart!

Ah, if my fire could ever again be extinguished,
What sorrow and pain I would have!
  Love, you possess my heart,
  Nothing from here below <note>referring to earthly or worldly distractions</note> can reach me.
  Increase, increase my fires forever;
If you were to consume me, how happy I would be!

<summary>A devotional poem expressing the soul's desire for an eternal and consuming divine love that transcends earthly attachments. It is part of a series of emblems where "Love" represents the Divine.</summary>
<keywords>divine love, immortality, spiritual fire, Otto van Veen, emblems, devotion</keywords>

### L I X.

<margin>page 115.</margin>

->*The goal of Love is to make one out of two.*<-

The end of a chaste Love is entire Unity;
The Beloved and her Lover become the same thing.
It is more; a metamorphosis
Truly transforms the Beloved into her Lover.
There is no longer any need here for quivers or arrows:
Love has cast off his blindfold;
And by a new miracle
He enters the heart without making any breaches <note>The author suggests that divine love does not wound the soul like earthly passion, but permeates it entirely.</note>.

Let us look at the path by which the soul has passed:
What rocks, what precipices,
What agitations, labors, and torments!
But finally, her heart has passed away <note>original: "trépassé" — here meaning both to die to oneself and to cross over into the divine.</note> into Love.

->O worthy and blessed passing!<-
->O death entirely delicious<-
->For this beautiful, loving soul,<-
->Who would not desire you?<-

This passing is the happy passage
Which gives this Beloved a share
In all the rights of her Spouse <note>A common mystical reference to the "Spiritual Marriage" between the soul and God.</note>:
You do more, Love, by transforming her into yourself.

<summary>Emblem 59 explores the mystical union of the soul with the Divine, describing a spiritual metamorphosis where the lover and beloved become one through a "delicious" death to the self.</summary>
<keywords>mystical union, spiritual marriage, divine love, transformation, soul's journey, unity</keywords>

# FROM O. VENIUS <note>Otto Vaenius (1556–1629), also known as Octavius Vaenius, a Dutch painter and humanist known for his "Emblems of Divine Love."</note>

## LX.

<margin>p. 116.</margin>

### *It is the fulfillment of the Law.* <note>original: "C'est de la Loi la consommation." A reference to Romans 13:10, "Love is the fulfilling of the law."</note>

Pure Love is therefore the end of all laws;
It encloses them within itself, far from excluding them:
    The soul, rising above nature,
    No longer has either will or choice.

For a long time, its lost will—
    Drowned in pure and naked charity <note><term>charity</term>: from the Latin 'caritas,' meaning the highest form of selfless, divine love.</note>—
Left it no use of itself;
    Love was then its law.

But since Love has transformed the soul into itself,
    It has changed its destiny;
    The soul obeys and commands in its turn:
Its will within Love is a supreme will;
Look no longer at this Beloved in herself,
    Behold only her Love.

Let us not amuse ourselves with the exterior, with the husk;
    That would be a vain lure:
    But let us penetrate to the very core,
And no longer distinguish between these most happy Lovers. <note>The "Lovers" refers to the mystical union between the Soul and the Divine.</note>

    Here all activity ceases;
    It is neither sorrow nor caress:
    One exists in a perfect rest:
Everything ends at last in the Sabbath of the Most High. <note><term>Sabbath of the Most High</term>: a state of eternal spiritual rest and union with God, transcending earthly labor.</note>

V 2
<meta>catchword: EPI-</meta>

<summary>This poem describes the mystical transformation of the soul through "Pure Love," where the individual will is surrendered and eventually unified with the Divine. It concludes by defining this state of perfect rest as the spiritual Sabbath.</summary>
<keywords>Pure Love, divine union, Otto Vaenius, mysticism, spiritual Sabbath, transformation of the soul, charity</keywords>

# EPILOGUE.

You, delight of the pure soul,
Love, who penetrates the heart
Having overcome nature
By your pure and your chaste ardor;

Simple light, inaccessible,
Sovereign Giver of every good,
You who make the heart unyielding <note>In this context, "inflexible" refers to a heart steadfast in its devotion to God, unmoved by worldly distractions.</note>
By plunging it into its own nothingness;

Child who governs the world,
To whom I consecrate these verses;
By a grace without equal
Spread them throughout this universe:

May all come to know you,
But even more to love you
As the sole Author of all being;
Make them prize <term>PURE LOVE</term> <note>original: "AMOUR PUR." A central concept in 17th-century mysticism (Quietism), referring to loving God for His own sake, without hope of reward or fear of punishment.</note>.

Ah, make them love you without division
With a disinterested love;
Make them understand my language,
Love, yes, you have heard my prayer.

I feel their hearts stirring,
And presenting themselves to your arrows;
May your pure and naked truth
Strike them according to my wishes:

I have no more wishes but for your glory,
I desire nothing for myself:
Deign to carry the victory,
Divine Child, become their King:

Strike them even while I amuse them;
And let their diversion
Be to surrender themselves without excuse
To your tiny, all-powerful arm <note>A reference to the Christ Child, a common image in the French School of Spirituality.</note>.

You know well for whom I implore you,
Nothing could be hidden from you.
O You, whom I love and whom I adore,
Make their hearts detached from everything.

<vocab>EPILOGUE, Love, PURE LOVE, Divine Child, pure soul, disinterested</vocab>

<summary>This epilogue serves as a final prayer to Divine Love, personified as the Christ Child, asking that the readers' hearts be moved toward a state of "pure love" and total detachment from the self.</summary>
<keywords>Pure Love, Quietism, Divine Child, mysticism, disinterested love, spiritual detachment, French School of Spirituality</keywords>

--- Page 436 ---
<meta>This page concludes the poetic work, serving as a final prayer and an address to the reader regarding the spiritual nature of the preceding verses.</meta>

# EPILOGUE.

May they seek you for yourself alone
Without thinking of their own interest;
Surrendering themselves to the supreme will,
May they never withdraw from it.
Fix the inconstancy of man,
Teach him your secret paths;
Let him know your wisdom <note>original: "sapience"</note>,
And surrender himself to your holy decrees.
Finally, be the soul of their soul;
Grant such grace to my song
That it produces in them that flame
Which comes from you, Divine Child!
If your Spouse was faithful,
If her heart hopes only in you,
If your love is eternal,
Favor her faith in this.
She has sung of her adventure <note>In this context, "adventure" refers to the soul's spiritual journey or experience.</note>
In every song, in every way,
That Charity <term>Charity: the theological concept of selfless, divine love (Caritas)</term> without measure
Which surpasses all other gifts.
She depicts there your caresses
And a thousand chaste delights,
So many mutual tendernesses
By which the senses are enchanted.
Do not believe, faithful people,
That these are merely songs:
Beneath these new figures <note>The author refers to the use of metaphorical or allegorical imagery.</note>
There are excellent lessons.
Receive through the divine Master
From my hand these little presents:
As a reward, please be
Simple and little Children.

->THE END.<-

<note>Vocabulary: Epilogue, Charity, Wisdom, faithful, Child, adventure</note>

<summary>The author concludes the work with a prayer for the reader's spiritual constancy and explains that the romantic imagery used throughout the poems represents the chaste, divine love between the soul and God.</summary>
<keywords>epilogue, divine love, spiritual childhood, allegory, charity, wisdom, soul's journey</keywords>

XXVI. I will rise, I will go about the city; and I will seek in the streets and in the public squares him who is the beloved of my soul: I sought him, and I found him not. <note>Song of Solomon 3:2</note> 27

XXVII. Have you not seen him whom my soul loves? When I had passed but a little way beyond them, I found him whom my soul loves: I hold him; and I will not let him go. <note>Song of Solomon 3:3-4</note> 28

XXVIII. But as for me, my whole good is to keep myself united to God, and to put all my hope in the Lord, my God. <note>Psalm 73:28; the text cites Psalm 72 following the Vulgate numbering</note> 29

XXIX. I rested under the shadow of him whom I had so greatly desired. <note>Song of Solomon 2:3</note> 30

XXX. How could we sing the songs of the Lord in a foreign land? <note>Psalm 137:4; cited as Psalm 136</note> 31

# BOOK III.

### THE SIGHS OF THE LOVING SOUL.
<note>The "Loving Soul" (L'Ame Amante) is a standard figure in devotional literature, representing the human spirit's yearning for divine union.</note>

XXXI. I adjure you, O daughters of Jerusalem, if you find my Beloved, to tell him that I languish with love. <note>Song of Solomon 5:8</note> 32

XXXII. Sustain me with flowers, strengthen me with apples: because I languish with love. <note>Song of Solomon 2:5</note> 33

XXXIII. My Beloved is mine, and I am his. He feeds among the lilies, until the day begins to break, and the shadows gradually fade away. <note>Song of Solomon 2:16-17</note> 34

XXXIV. I am my Beloved's, and his heart turns toward me. <note>Song of Solomon 7:10</note> 35

XXXV. My soul melted as soon as my Beloved spoke. <note>Song of Solomon 5:6</note> 36

XXXVI. For what is there for me in heaven, and what do I desi—
